Invariance principles and Log-distance of F-KPP fronts in a random medium
Alexander Drewitz, Lars Schmitz

Abstract
We study the front of the solution to the F-KPP equation with randomized non-linearity. Under suitable assumptions on the randomness involving spatial mixing behavior and boundedness, we show that the front of the solution lags at most logarithmically in time behind the front of the solution of the corresponding linearized equation, i.e. the parabolic Anderson model. This can be interpreted as a partial generalization of Bramson’s findings [ 9 ] for the homogeneous setting. Building on this result, we establish functional central limit theorems for the fronts of the solutions to both equations.



Preliminary version

1
Introduction and main results

1.1
The classical F-KPP equation

The F-KPP equation is the initial value problem given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}w_{t}(t,x)&=\frac{1}{2}w_{xx}(t,x)+w(t,x)(1-w(t,x)),\quad t>0,\ x\in\mathbb{R},\\
w(0,x)&=w_{0}(x),\quad x\in\mathbb{R},\end{split}

(1.1)

with initial condition
w_{0}:\mathbb{R}\to[0,1].
Its investigation has a long history, with seminal results dating back to Fisher
[
13
]
and Kolmogorov-Petrovskii-Piskunov
[
24
]
. Their research had been motivated by pioneering works in genetics, where the equation has been used to model a randomly mating diploid population living in a one-dimensional habitat. Further applications can be found in chemical combustion theory or flame propagation, see
[
1
]
,
[
12
]
, as well as
[
36
]
and references therein.

In
[
24
]
it has been shown that for reasonably general non-linearities (see (
SC
) at the beginning of Section
1.4
for further details) and initial conditions, the solution to (
1.1
) approaches a
traveling wave

g:\mathbb{R}\to[0,1]
. I.e., there exists a function
m:[0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}
– generally referred to as the
front
or
breakpoint
– such that

w(t,\cdot+m(t))\displaystyle\mathop{\longrightarrow}_{t\rightarrow\infty}g\quad\text{uniformly.}

The limit
g
is known (see
[
24
, Theorems 14 and 17]
) to solve the differential equation

\frac{1}{2}g^{\prime\prime}(x)+\sqrt{2}g^{\prime}(x)+g(x)(1-g(x))=0,\ x\in\mathbb{R},

0<g(x)<1\quad\text{for all }x\in\mathbb{R}\quad\text{ and }\quad g(x)\displaystyle\mathop{\longrightarrow}_{x\rightarrow\infty}0,\ g(x)\displaystyle\mathop{\longrightarrow}_{x\rightarrow-\infty}1,

and it is unique up to spatial translations. Additionally, for the important case of Heaviside-type initial condition
w_{0}=\mathds{1}_{(-\infty,0]},
in
[
24
]
the first order asymptotics
m(t)/t\displaystyle\mathop{\longrightarrow}_{t\rightarrow\infty}\sqrt{2}
has been derived.
A couple of decades later, Bramson in his seminal work
[
9
]
improved this result by computing the second order correction up to additive constants.
More precisely, he showed that for each
\varepsilon\in(0,1),
the choice
m(t)=m^{\varepsilon}(t):=\sup\{x\in\mathbb{R}:w(t,x)=\varepsilon\}
fulfills

m(t)=\sqrt{2}t-\frac{3}{2\sqrt{2}}\ln t+\mathcal{O}(1)\quad\text{ as }t\to\infty.

(1.2)

Later on, Bramson
[
8
, Theorem 3]
generalized this result to more general initial conditions; roughly speaking, he demanded the initial condition
w_{0}(x)
to have a sufficiently fast exponential decay for
x\to\infty
and to be non-vanishing for
x\to-\infty
.
One of the main tools employed in the proof was the McKean representation of the solution to (
1.1
) in terms of expectations of branching Brownian motion, see
[
20
]
and
[
27
]
.
Another important ingredient was the comparison of the solution of (
1.1
) to the solution of its linearized version

\displaystyle\begin{split}u_{t}(t,x)&=\frac{1}{2}u_{xx}(t,x)+u(t,x),\quad t>0,\ x\in\mathbb{R},\\
u(0,x)&=w_{0}(x).\end{split}

(1.3)

Indeed, since
m(t)
describes the front of the solution where
u
is small and hence
1-u\approx 1,
the heuristics is that
m(t)
is in some sense well-approximated by the front of the solution to (
1.3
).
More precisely, for
\overline{m}(t):=\sup\{x\in\mathbb{R}:u(t,x)=\varepsilon\}
and Heaviside-type initial condition
w_{0}=\mathds{1}_{(-\infty,0]}
, standard Gaussian computations entail

\overline{m}(t)=\sqrt{2}t-\frac{1}{2\sqrt{2}}\ln t+\mathcal{O}(1).

(1.4)

In combination with (
1.2
), this results in a respective logarithmic backlog of the two fronts in the sense that

\overline{m}(t)-m(t)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\ln t+\mathcal{O}(1).

(1.5)

The main goal of this article is to investigate the effect of introducing a random potential in the non-linearity of (
1.1
) as well as in its linearization (
1.3
),
on the logarithmic backlog derived in (
1.5
) (cf. Theorem
1.4
). Taking advantage of this result we will then also derive functional central limit theorems for the fronts of the respective solutions to the randomized equations, see Theorem
1.3
and Corollary
1.5
below, which can be interpreted as analogues to homogeneous-case results (
1.4
) and (
1.2
).

1.2
The randomized F-KPP equation and the parabolic Anderson model

Already in Fisher’s seminal paper
[
13
]
, where he investigated the setting (
1.1
) of homogeneous branching rates, it has been observed that a more realistic model would be obtained by considering spatially heterogeneous rates of the transformation of recessive to advantageous alleles. This, alongside a mathematical interest, is our guiding motivation to consider the setting of random
\xi
. Replacing the term
w(1-w)
of (
1.1
) by a more general non-linearity
F(w)
fulfilling suitable
standard conditions
(see (
SC
) below), and overriding the notation
u
and
w
from the homogeneous setting of the previous section, we then arrive at the equation

\displaystyle\begin{split}w_{t}&=\frac{1}{2}w_{xx}+\xi(x,\omega)\cdot F(w),\quad t>0,\ x\in\mathbb{R},\\
w(0,x)&=w_{0}(x),\quad x\in\mathbb{R},\end{split}

(F-KPP)

as well as its linearized version, the
parabolic Anderson model

\displaystyle\begin{split}u_{t}&=\frac{1}{2}u_{xx}+\xi(x,\omega)\cdot u,\quad t>0,\ x\in\mathbb{R},\\
u(0,x)&=u_{0}(x),\quad x\in\mathbb{R}.\end{split}

(PAM)

Here, the stochastic process
\xi:\mathbb{R}\times\Omega\to(0,\infty)
models the random medium. For the case
F(w)=w(1-w)
and degenerate
\xi\equiv 1
, these two equations yield the special cases (
1.1
) and (
1.3
), respectively.
It has long been known, see e.g. Freidlin
[
14
, Theorem 7.6.1]
, that under suitable assumptions there exists
v_{0}>0
, such that the solution
w(t,x)
to (
F-KPP
) converges to
0
(resp.
1
), uniformly for all
x\geq\overline{v}t
with
\overline{v}>v_{0}
(resp. for all
x\leq\underline{v}t
with
\underline{v}<v_{0}
), as
t
tends to infinity. In a similar way, this result can be shown to hold for the solution to (
PAM
) with the same
v_{0}
as well, showing that the
speeds
or
velocities
of both fronts, the one of the solution to (
F-KPP
) as well as the one of the solution to (
PAM
), coincide.
Consequently, as in the homogeneous case, the question of second order corrections arises naturally.

1.3
Summary of results

In order to address this question and to be able to summarize our results, we introduce some notation.
Let
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
and
a>0
. Furthermore, write
u=u^{\xi,u_{0}}
for the solution to (
PAM
) with initial condition
u_{0},
and
w=w^{\xi,F,w_{0}}
for the solution to (
F-KPP
) with initial condition
w_{0}
. As in the previous section, the fronts of the respective solutions, denoted by

\begin{split}\overline{m}^{\xi,u_{0},a}(t)&:=\sup\big\{x\in\mathbb{R}:u(t,x)\geq a\big\},\\
m^{\xi,F,w_{0},\varepsilon}(t)&:=\sup\big\{x\in\mathbb{R}:w(t,x)\geq\varepsilon\big\},\end{split}

(1.6)

are of special interest. Since we will from now on focus on the heterogeneous setting, we override the notation from Section
1.1
, where it was used to denote the fronts in the homogeneous case, and define

\displaystyle\begin{split}\overline{m}(t)&:=\overline{m}^{a}(t):=\overline{m}^{\xi,\mathds{1}_{(-\infty,0]},a}(t),\\
m(t)&:=m^{\varepsilon}(t):=m^{\xi,F,\mathds{1}_{(-\infty,0]},\varepsilon}(t).\end{split}

(1.7)

Our findings are motivated by the respective results
of (
1.4
), (
1.2
), and
(
1.5
) for the homogeneous case, which provide information about the position of the fronts of the solutions to the respective equations, and thus their respective backlog as well. Under suitable assumptions, our results are summarized in the following two statements:

(a)

There exist a constant
C\in(0,\infty)
and a
\mathbb{P}
-a.s. finite random time
\mathcal{T}(\omega)
such that for all
t\geq\mathcal{T}(\omega),

\overline{m}(t)-m(t)\leq C\ln t;

(1.8)

see Theorem
1.4
below.

(b)

After centering and diffusive rescaling, the stochastic processes
[0,\infty)\ni t\to\overline{m}(t)
and
[0,\infty)\ni t\to m(t)
satisfy invariance principles; see Theorem
1.3
and Corollary
1.5
below.

As is shown in the companion article
[
11
]
, in a certain sense there is a logarithmic lower bound for
\overline{m}(t)-m(t)
corresponding to (
1.8
) as well; cf. also Section
1.7
below for further discussion.

1.4
Further notation

In order to be able to precisely formulate the previously summarized results, we have to introduce some further notation. We start with introducing the
standard conditions
for the non-linearity, i.e.,
F
in (
F-KPP
) has to fulfill the following:

\begin{split}&F\in C^{1}([0,1]),\quad F(0)=F(1)=0,\quad F(w)>0\quad\forall w\in(0,1),\quad\\
F^{\prime}&(0)=1=\sup_{w>0}F(w)w^{-1},\quad F^{\prime}(1)<0,\quad\limsup_{w\downarrow 0}\frac{1-F^{\prime}(w)}{w}<\infty.\end{split}

(SC)

Figure 1:
Sketches of functions fulfilling (
SC
), all of which are dominated by the identity function.

We will now specify the classes of initial conditions under consideration for both, (
F-KPP
) and (
PAM
). For this purpose, we
fix
\delta^{\prime}\in(0,1)
and
C^{\prime}>1,
and require an initial condition
u_{0}
of (
PAM
) to fulfill

\begin{split}&\delta^{\prime}\mathds{1}_{[-\delta^{\prime},0]}\leq u_{0}\leq C^{\prime}\mathds{1}_{(-\infty,0]}.\end{split}

(PAM-INI)

Our results also hold for initial conditions that decay sufficiently fast at infinity, as well as for initial conditions that grow towards minus infinity with sufficiently small exponential rate.
However, in order to avoid further technical complications we stick to the above set of initial conditions.

In addition, let us introduce a tail condition for the initial condition of (
F-KPP
), which is the same as the one for the case
\xi\equiv 1
stated in
[
8
, (1.17)]
. For this purpose,
we fix
N,N^{\prime}>0
, and require
w_{0}
as in (
F-KPP
) to fulfill

\begin{split}0\leq w_{0}\leq\mathds{1}_{(-\infty,0]}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\int_{[x-N,x]}w_{0}(y)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y\geq\delta^{\prime}\quad\forall x\leq-N^{\prime}.\end{split}

(KPP-INI)

Denote by
\mathcal{S}^{1}
the class of functions
f:\mathbb{R}\to[0,\infty)
which are pointwise limits of increasing sequences of continuous functions, and let

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{\text{PAM}}

\displaystyle:=\big\{u_{0}\in\mathcal{S}^{1}:\ u_{0}\text{ fulfills \eqref{eq:PAM_initial}}\big\},\quad\mathcal{I}_{\text{F-KPP}}:=\big\{w_{0}\in\mathcal{S}^{1}:\ w_{0}\text{ fulfills \eqref{eq:KPP_initial}}\big\},

which will be the classes of initial conditions under consideration.
An emblematic example which is contained in both,
\mathcal{I}_{\text{F-KPP}}
and
\mathcal{I}_{\text{PAM}},
is the function
\mathds{1}_{(-\infty,0]}
of Heaviside type.

We will assume
\xi=(\xi(x))_{x\in\mathbb{R}}
to be a stochastic process on a probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
having Hölder continuous paths, i.e., there exists
\alpha=\alpha(\xi)>0
and
C=C(\xi)>0
, such that

\left|\xi(x)-\xi(y)\right|\leq C\,|x-y|^{\alpha}\quad\forall x,y\in\mathbb{R},

(HÖL)

and such that the following conditions are fulfilled:

•

\xi
is
uniformly bounded away from
0
and
\infty
, i.e., there exist constants
0<\texttt{ei}\leq\texttt{es}<\infty
such that
\mathbb{P}
-a.s.,

\texttt{ei}\leq\xi(x)\leq\texttt{es}\quad\text{ for all }x\in\mathbb{R};

(BDD)

•

\xi
is
stationary
, i.e. for every
h\in\mathbb{R}

we have

(\xi(x))_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\overset{d}{=}(\xi(x+h))_{x\in\mathbb{R}};

(STAT)

•

\xi
fulfills a
\psi
-mixing
condition: Let
\mathcal{F}_{x}:=\sigma(\xi(z):z\leq x)
and
\mathcal{F}^{y}:=\sigma(\xi(z):z\geq y)
,
x,y\in\mathbb{R}
and assume that there is a continuous, non-increasing function
\psi:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
, such that for all
j,k\in\mathbb{Z}
with

j\leq k
as well as
X\in\mathcal{L}^{1}(\Omega,\mathcal{F}_{j},\mathbb{P})
and
Y\in\mathcal{L}^{1}(\Omega,\mathcal{F}^{k},\mathbb{P})
we have

\displaystyle\big|\mathbb{E}\big[X-\mathbb{E}[X]\,|\,\mathcal{F}^{k}\big]\big|

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}[|X|]\cdot\psi(k-j),

\displaystyle\big|\mathbb{E}\big[Y-\mathbb{E}[Y]\,|\,\mathcal{F}_{j}\big]\big|

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}[|Y|]\cdot\psi({k-j}),

(MIX)

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}

\displaystyle\psi(k)<\infty.

Note that (
•
‣
1.4
) implies the ergodicity of
\xi
with respect to the shift operator
\theta_{y}
acting via
\xi(\cdot)\circ\theta_{y}=\xi(\cdot+y)
,
y\in\mathbb{R}
.

Summarizing, we arrive at the following standing assumptions:

\begin{gathered}\text{We will assume
conditions \eqref{eq:hoelder_cont}, \eqref{eq:POT}, \eqref{eq:STAT} and \eqref{eq:MIX}}\\
\text{to be fulfilled from now on, if not explicitly mentioned otherwise.}\end{gathered}

(
Standing assumptions
)

Since we allow for non-smooth initial conditions in both equations, (
F-KPP
) and (
PAM
), we shortly comment on the notion of solution in this setting. We call a function
w
to (
F-KPP
) a
generalized solution to (
F-KPP
)
, if it satisfies

w(t,x)=E_{x}\Big[\exp\Big\{\int_{0}^{t}\xi(B_{s})F(w(t-s,B_{s}))/w(t-s,B_{s})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s\Big\}\,w_{0}(B_{t})\Big],\quad\forall\ (t,x)\in[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R},

(1.9)

where
E_{x}
is the corresponding expectation of the probability measure
P_{x}
, under which
(B_{t})_{t\geq 0}
is a standard Brownian motion starting in
x\in\mathbb{R}
.
This equation can be interpreted as a mild formulation of (
F-KPP
) and one can show (see e.g.
[
14
, (1.4), p. 354, and (a), p. 355]
) that every classical solution to (
F-KPP
) also is a generalized one. Generalized solutions can be shown to exist under weak assumptions, and, vice versa, in many instances they turn out to be classical solutions indeed, see Proposition
A.9
in Appendix
A
.

Remark 1.1
.

A useful observation is that for initial conditions in
\mathcal{I}_{\text{F-KPP}}
, generalized solutions can be approximated by classical solutions. That is, if
(w_{0}^{(n)})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\mathcal{I}_{\text{F-KPP}}
is a sequence of continuous functions which increase pointwise to
w_{0}
, then by Corollary
A.11
the corresponding sequence of (by Proposition
A.9
classical) solutions
(w^{(n)})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}=(w^{w_{0}^{(n)}})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
to (
F-KPP
) is also monotone and thus the limit
w(t,x):=\lim_{n\to\infty}w^{{(n)}}(t,x)
exists for all
(t,x)\in[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}
. Dominated convergence and the fact that
w^{(n)}
also is a generalized solution then imply

\displaystyle w(t,x)

\displaystyle=\lim_{n\to\infty}w^{(n)}(t,x)=\lim_{n\to\infty}E_{x}\Big[\exp\Big\{\int_{0}^{t}\xi(B_{s})\frac{F(w^{(n)}(t-s,B_{s}))}{w^{(n)}(t-s,B_{s})}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s\Big\}\,w^{(n)}_{0}(B_{t})\Big]

\displaystyle=E_{x}\Big[\exp\Big\{\int_{0}^{t}\xi(B_{s})\frac{F(w(t-s,B_{s}))}{w(t-s,B_{s})}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s\Big\}\,w_{0}(B_{t})\Big].

I.e.,
w
is the generalized solution to (
F-KPP
) with initial condition
w_{0}
.

A similar concept is possible for the solution to (
PAM
) as well. That is, for
u_{0}\in\mathcal{I}_{\text{PAM}}
, we call a function

\displaystyle u(t,x)=E_{x}\Big[\exp\Big\{\int_{0}^{t}\xi(B_{s})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s\Big\}u_{0}(B_{t})\Big],

(1.10)

a
generalized solution to (
PAM
)
.
Note that this is an explicit expression of the solution in terms of a Brownian path, while in (
1.9
) the solution is only given implicitly.
Furthermore, if
u_{0}
is continuous, then by
[
21
, Remark 4.4.4]
, there exists a unique solution
u
to (
PAM
) which fulfills
u\in C^{1,2}((0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R})
and
u(0,\cdot)=u_{0}.

As a consequence of these observations, in the following, we will always consider generalized solutions.

1.5
The linearized equation

As already mentioned in the first section, we expect that investigating the solution to (
PAM
) might also provide some insight into the solution to (
F-KPP
). Therefore, starting with the first order of the front as a function of time, it turns out useful to consider the so-called
Lyapunov exponent

\Lambda(v):=\lim_{t\to\infty}\frac{1}{t}\ln u(t,vt).

(1.11)

Due to Proposition
A.3
, the Lyapunov exponent exists
\mathbb{P}
-a.s. for all
v\in\mathbb{R}
, is non-random, and – as a consequence of Corollary
3.10
– does not depend on the initial condition in
\mathcal{I}_{\text{PAM}}
under consideration.
Furthermore, the function
[0,\infty)\ni v\mapsto\Lambda(v)
is concave, tends to
-\infty
as
v\to\infty
and
\Lambda(0)=\texttt{es}
, where
es
is defined in (
BDD
).
\Lambda(v)
describes the asymptotic exponential growth of the solution in the linear regime with speed
v
. By Proposition
A.3
, there exists a unique
v_{0}>0
, such that

\Lambda(v_{0})=0,

which we will call
velocity
or
speed
of the solution to (
PAM
).
Using the properties of the Lyapunov exponent, we immediately infer the first order asymptotics for
\overline{m}
to
\mathbb{P}\text{-a.s.}
satisfy

\frac{\overline{m}(t)}{t}\displaystyle\mathop{\longrightarrow}_{t\rightarrow\infty}v_{0}

It will turn out that our methods work if we require
v_{0}
to be strictly larger than some “critical” value
v_{c}
, defined in Lemma
2.4
(d). Roughly speaking, the condition
v>v_{c}
allows us to find a suitable additive tilting parameter in the exponent of the Feynman-Kac representation (
1.10
), which depends on
v
and makes the solution
u(t,x)
to (
PAM
) amenable to the investigation by standard tools for values
x\approx vt
and large
t
. Hence, we work under the assumption

v_{0}>v_{c}.

(VEL)

As will be shown in Section
4.4
, this assumption is fulfilled for a rich class of potentials
\xi.

We start with investigating the fluctuations of the function
t\mapsto\ln u(t,vt)
around
t\Lambda(v)
for values
v
in a neighborhood of
v_{0}
,
which are interesting in their own right. To this end, on the space
C([0,\infty))
of continuous functions from
[0,\infty)
to
\mathbb{R},
we define the metric

\rho(f,g):=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}2^{-j}\frac{\lVert f-g\rVert_{j}}{1+\lVert f-g\rVert_{j}},\quad f,g\in C([0,\infty)),

(1.12)

where we write
\lVert f-g\rVert_{j}:=\sup_{x\in[0,j]}|f(x)-g(x)|.

This makes
(C([0,\infty)),\rho)
a complete separable metric space.

Theorem 1.2
.

Let (
VEL
) be fulfilled,
u_{0}\in\mathcal{I}_{\text{\emph{PAM}}},
and
u=u^{\xi,u_{0}}
be the corresponding solution to (
PAM
). Furthermore, let
V\subset(v_{c},\infty)
be a compact interval such that
v_{0}\in\text{\emph{int}}(V)
. Then for each
v\in V
, as
n\to\infty
the sequence of random variables
(nv)^{-1/2}\big(\ln u(n,vn)-n\Lambda(v)\big),

n\in\mathbb{N},
converges in
\mathbb{P}
-distribution to a centered Gaussian random variable with variance
\sigma_{v}^{2}\in[0,\infty)
, where
\sigma_{v}^{2}
is defined in (
3.1
). If
\sigma_{v}^{2}>0
, the sequence of processes

[0,\infty)\ni t\mapsto\frac{1}{\sqrt{nv\sigma_{v}^{2}}}\big(\ln u(nt,vnt)-nt\Lambda(v)\big),\quad n\in\mathbb{N},

converges as
n\to\infty
in
\mathbb{P}
-distribution to a standard Brownian motion in the sense of weak convergence of measures on
C([0,\infty))
endowed with the metric
\rho
from (
1.12
).

In combination with perturbation estimates for
u,
we will use this result in order to infer an invariance principle for the front of the solution to (
PAM
). Note that since the function
t\mapsto\overline{m}_{t}
may be discontinuous, we consider convergence in the Skorohod space
D([0,\infty))
in the following result.

Theorem 1.3
.

Let (
VEL
) be fulfilled,
u_{0}\in\mathcal{I}_{\text{\emph{PAM}}}
and
M>0
. Then for
n\to\infty,
the sequence
n^{-1/2}\big(\overline{m}^{\xi,u_{0},M}(n)-v_{0}n\big),

n\in\mathbb{N},
converges in
\mathbb{P}
-distribution to a centered Gaussian random variable with variance
\widetilde{\sigma}_{v_{0}}^{2}\in[0,\infty)
, where
\widetilde{\sigma}_{v_{0}}^{2}
is defined in (
3.57
). If
\widetilde{\sigma}_{v_{0}}^{2}>0
, the sequence of processes

[0,\infty)\ni t\mapsto\frac{\overline{m}^{\xi,u_{0},M}(nt)-v_{0}nt}{\sqrt{n\widetilde{\sigma}_{v_{0}}^{2}}},\quad n\in\mathbb{N},

converges as
n\to\infty
in
\mathbb{P}
-distribution to a standard Brownian motion in the Skorohod space
D([0,\infty))
.

We underline that in the above theorems the case
\sigma_{v}^{2}=0
(resp.
\widetilde{\sigma}_{v_{0}}^{2}=0
) is allowed and leads to a degenerate limit of the corresponding sequences. This can be excluded, e.g. if the finite-dimensional projections of the stochastic process
\xi=(\xi(x))_{x\in\mathbb{R}}
are associated (see e.g.
[
31
]
or
[
30
]
for definitions and results). In this case the covariances in (
3.1
) are nonnegative and
\sigma_{v}^{2}>0
follows.
[
28
, Proposition 2.1]
provides an example of a potential, which is generated by an i.i.d. sequence of random variables and thus associated.

1.6
The non-linear equation

Coming back to the original equation of interest, it is natural to ask whether we can obtain results for (
F-KPP
), which are in some sense counterparts to those derived in latter section for (
PAM
). For the solution to (
F-KPP
) it is known (see e.g.
[
14
, § 7.6]
) that the first order of the front is linear as well and moves with the same velocity as the front of (
PAM
). Indeed, by
[
14
, Theorem 7.6.1]
we have that
\mathbb{P}\text{-a.s.},

\frac{m(t)}{t}\displaystyle\mathop{\longrightarrow}_{t\rightarrow\infty}v_{0}.

As mentioned in Section
1.2
, the next result, which is one of the main results of the paper, states that there is an at most logarithmic distance of the fronts of (
F-KPP
) and (
PAM
).

Theorem 1.4
.

Let (
VEL
) be fulfilled. Then for each
F
fulfilling (
SC
) there exists a constant
>0
such that the following holds: For all
M>0
,
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
,
u_{0}\in\mathcal{I}_{\text{\emph{PAM}}}
and
w_{0}\in\mathcal{I}_{\text{\emph{F-KPP}}},
there exists a non-random
C=C(\varepsilon,M,u_{0},w_{0})>0
and a
\mathbb{P}\text{-a.s.}
finite random time
\mathcal{T}=\mathcal{T}(\xi,\varepsilon,M,u_{0},w_{0})\geq 0
, such that

-C\leq\overline{m}^{\xi,u_{0},M}(t)-m^{\xi,F,w_{0},\varepsilon}(t)\leq C_{1}\ln t+C\quad\forall t\geq\mathcal{T}.

(1.13)

Moreover for
w_{0}=u_{0}
and
M\leq\varepsilon
, the left inequality in (
1.13
) is zero for all
t\geq 0
.

Furthermore, combining Theorem
1.3
and Theorem
1.4
, we can deduce an invariance principle for the front of (
F-KPP
) as well.

Corollary 1.5
.

Let (
VEL
) be fulfilled,
F
fulfill (
SC
),
w_{0}\in\mathcal{I}_{\text{\emph{F-KPP}}}
and
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
. Then as
n\to\infty,
the sequence
n^{-1/2}\big(m^{\xi,F,w_{0},\varepsilon}(n)-v_{0}n\big),

n\in\mathbb{N},
converges in
\mathbb{P}
-distribution to a centered Gaussian random variable with variance
\widetilde{\sigma}_{v_{0}}^{2}\in[0,\infty)
, where
\widetilde{\sigma}_{v_{0}}^{2}
is defined in (
3.57
). If
\widetilde{\sigma}_{v_{0}}^{2}>0
, the sequence of processes

[0,\infty)\ni t\mapsto\frac{m^{\xi,F,w_{0},\varepsilon}(nt)-v_{0}nt}{\sqrt{n\widetilde{\sigma}_{v_{0}}^{2}}},\quad n\in\mathbb{N},

converges as
n\to\infty
in
\mathbb{P}
-distribution to a standard Brownian motion in the Skorohod space
D([0,\infty))
.

1.7
Discussion and previous results

As already alluded to above, in the homogeneous case (
1.1
) of
constant potential the front has been well-understood by now. This is indeed the case to a much
wider extent than illustrated in the Introduction, see e.g.
[
6
]
and references therein for further details. Also the heterogeneous setting (
F-KPP
) of random potential and the properties of its solution have been investigated. Specifically, under fairly general assumptions, the existence and characterization of the propagation speed (i.e., the linear order
\lim_{t\to\infty}m(t)/t
of the position of the front) have been derived by Freidlin and Gärtner, see e.g.
[
15
]
as well as
[
14
, Chapter VII]
, using large deviation principles. Incidentally, the Feynman-Kac formula (see Proposition
2.3
below), which characterizes the solution to the linearization (
PAM
), has also played an important role in the derivation.

Second order corrections for the position have been investigated by Nolen
[
28
]
. Also making the detour along (
PAM
), he examines (
F-KPP
) for a potential
\xi
under similar assumptions, but requires the (random) initial conditions to satisfy

\mathcal{C}_{1}(\xi)g^{\xi,\gamma}(x)\leq w_{0}(x,\xi)\leq\mathcal{C}_{2}(\xi)g^{\xi,\gamma}(x)\quad\forall\ x>0.

(1.14)

Here,
\mathcal{C}_{1}(\xi)
and
\mathcal{C}_{2}(\xi)
are positive random variables, and
g=g^{\xi,\gamma}
is a solution to

g^{\prime\prime}(x)+(\xi(x)-\gamma)g(x)=0,\ x>0

for
\gamma>\overline{\gamma}
, for a certain
\overline{\gamma}>0.

For technical reasons, he additionally requires
\gamma<\gamma^{*}
, i.e., the initial condition
w_{0}(x)
must not decay too fast as
x
tends to infinity. The technical assumption (
1.14
) thus entails being in the
supercritical regime
which corresponds to waves that move faster than the minimal speed. As his main result, Nolen obtains a central limit theorem for
m(t)
in this case also, see
[
28
, Theorem 1.4]
. In this lingo, our set of initial conditions corresponds to the
critical regime
, and
Corollary
1.5
(for the critical regime) also suggests that the randomness of the functional central limit theorem is already coming from the environment, and not necessarily due to the random initial condition.

Furthermore, in
[
29
]
a corresponding invariance principle for the front has been derived in the case where the non-linearity in (
F-KPP
) is either ignition typ or bistable.

In
[
18
]
, on the other hand, for the case of periodic instead of random
\xi,
the authors have investigated the respective logarithmic correction, corresponding to (
1.2
) in the homogeneous setting; here, the authors have been able to characterize the constant in front of the logarithmic correction as a certain minimizer.

In our main Theorem
1.4
, we establish a corresponding logarithmic upper bound for the difference (
1.5
), also in the setting of a random environment. However, our methods are currently too coarse for identifying a sharp prefactor. Nevertheless, it is a natural question whether the logarithmic upper bound we derive captures the correct order at least. And indeed, in some sense, a partial positive answer to this questions is provided in the companion article
[
11
]
. There, the authors show that even in the setting of (
F-KPP
) described above, for some potential satisfying our standing assumptions,
w_{0}=\mathds{1}_{(-\infty,0]},
and all
\varepsilon\in(0,1/2),
there exists a sequence
(t_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}=(t_{n}(\varepsilon))_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
such that
t_{n}\to\infty
and

m_{t_{n}}^{\varepsilon}-\inf\{x\geq 0:w(t_{n},x)\leq 1-\varepsilon\}\in\Omega(\ln n);

(1.15)

in particular, the solution does not exhibit a uniformly bounded transition front.

As explained above, there is a profound connection between the PDEs we consider and branching Brownian motion, which is more involved that in the setting of constant
\xi.
Indeed,
related results for branching random walk in random environment (BRWRE) have been recently derived in
[
10
]
. In this source the authors analyze the distribution of the maximal particle of a branching random walk in random environment, which itself is closely related to a discrete-space version of (
F-KPP
). In particular, a corresponding logarithmic upper bound on the distance of the expected position of the maximal particle and the median of the distribution of the maximal particle is given. Furthermore, an invariance principle for the median of the position of the maximal particle of BRWRE is derived. It is therefore no surprise that, on the one hand, principal techniques we employ in this paper are generalizations and adaptations of respective discrete space analogues from
[
10
]
. On the other hand, we work under weaker independence assumptions and less assumptions on the non-linearity which is assumed to be in (
SC
).
What is more, we also obtain a more profound understanding of the scope of the methods employed, see in particular Section
4.4
.
In combination with the current article, somewhat of a completion of the picture is provided by
[
11
]
, by establishing a result with a similar flavor of Bramson’s alluded to above.

Recall that directly before Theorem
1.2
we have introduced our assumption (
VEL
), which reads
v_{0}>v_{c};
from a technical point of view, this will be necessary for our change of measure argument to work. It is not hard to show that for a rich class of potentials, this condition is satisfied indeed, cf. Section
4.4
. What is more, however, in Proposition
4.10
below we also obtain a more profound understanding of the scope of the methods employed. I.e., there exist potentials
\xi
fulfilling (
Standing assumptions
), but such that
v_{0}<v_{c}
holds true. In this regime our methods do not apply, and it is an interesting and open question to obtain a more profound understand of this situation as well.

1.8
Notational conventions

We will frequently use sums of real-indexed quantities
A_{x}
,
x\in\mathbb{R}
. In this case, we write

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{x}A_{i}

\displaystyle:=\sum_{i=1}^{\lfloor x\rfloor}A_{i}+A_{x},\quad x\in[0,\infty)\setminus\mathbb{N}_{0},

where
\sum_{i=1}^{0}:=0
. This notion remains consistent if we also allow for additive constants
b\in\mathbb{R},
i.e.

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{x}(A_{i}+b)=\sum_{i=1}^{\lfloor x\rfloor}A_{i}+A_{x}+b\cdot x=\sum_{i=1}^{x}A_{i}+\sum_{i=1}^{x}b,\quad x\in[0,\infty).

Finally, we set

\displaystyle\sum_{i=x+1}^{y}A_{i}

\displaystyle:=\begin{cases}\sum_{i=1}^{y}A_{i}-\sum_{i=1}^{x}A_{i},&x\leq y,\\
\sum_{i=1}^{x}A_{i}-\sum_{i=1}^{y}A_{i},&x>y,\end{cases}\quad x,y\in[0,\infty).

A prime example is the quantity
A_{x}=\ln E_{x}\big[e^{\int_{0}^{H_{\lceil x\rceil-1}}(\xi(B_{s})-\texttt{es})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s}\big]
, where
H_{y}:=\inf\{t\geq 0:B_{t}=y\}
. Indeed, by the strong Markov property we have
\ln E_{x}\big[e^{\int_{0}^{H_{0}}(\xi(B_{s})-\texttt{es})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s}\big]=\sum_{i=1}^{\lfloor x\rfloor}A_{i}+A_{x}
for all
x\in[0,\infty)\setminus\mathbb{N}_{0}
.

Furthermore, we will often use positive finite constants
c_{1},c_{2},\ldots
in the proofs. This numbering is consistent within any of the proofs, and it is reset after each proof. On the
other hand,
C_{1},C_{2},\ldots
will be used to denote positive finite constants that are fixed throughout
the article, and they will oftentimes depend on each other.
Other constants like
c,C,\varepsilon,\delta
etc. in the proofs are used to compare certain quantities and are also reset after each proof.

Acknowledgments:
We would like to thank Jiří Černý for useful discussions and remarks on a preliminary version of this paper.

2
Some technical tools

In this section we will introduce some further tools that will be helpful in the proof of the main results.

2.1
Connection to branching processes

We define a
branching Brownian motion in random environment
(BBMRE) as follows: Conditionally on the realization of
\xi
and for fixed
x\in\mathbb{R}
, consider an initial particle starting at a point
x
and moving as a standard Brownian motion
(B_{t})_{t\geq 0}
on
\mathbb{R}
. While at site
y
, the particle dies at rate
\xi(y)
. More precisely, for an exponentially distributed random variable
S
with parameter one, independent of everything else, the first particle dies at time
\inf\big\{t\geq 0:S<\int_{0}^{t}\xi(B_{s})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s\big\}
.
When the initial particle dies, it gives birth to
k
new particles with probability
p_{k}
,
k\in\mathbb{N}
(see (
PROB
) below for the precise assumptions on the
p_{k}
). The new particle(s) start their evolution at the site where their parent particle had died, and they evolve independently of everything else and according to the same stochastic behavior as their parent. Note that on the one hand we assume
p_{0}=0
, so genealogies do not die out at any finite time. On the other hand, we allow
p_{1}>0,
i.e., it is possible to die and give birth to one descendant. This implies that a particle at site
y
branches into more than one particle with rate
\xi(y)(1-p_{1})
. We denote the corresponding probability measure by
\texttt{P}_{x}^{\xi}
and write
\texttt{E}_{x}^{\xi}
for the respective expectation.
By
N(t)
we denote the set of particles alive at time
t
. For
\nu\in N(t)
we let
(X_{s}^{\nu})_{s\in[0,t]}
be the spatial trajectory of the genealogy of ancestral particles of
\nu
up to time
t
. For a Borel set
A\subset\mathbb{R}
and
y\in\mathbb{R}
we define

\displaystyle N(t,A)

\displaystyle:=\big\{\nu\in N(t):X_{t}^{\nu}\in A\big\}\quad\text{ and }\quad N^{\leq}(t,y):=|N(t,(-\infty,y])|,

(2.1)

i.e., the set of particles which are in
A
at time
t
, and the number of particles to the left of
y
at time
t.

Let us now introduce a set of functions, formulated in terms of the probability generating function of the sequence
(p_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
as above. More precisely, assume
(p_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
and
F=F^{(p_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}}
to fulfill

\begin{split}p_{k}\in[0,1]\ \forall k\in\mathbb{N},&\quad\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}p_{k}=1,\quad\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}kp_{k}=2,\quad m_{2}:=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}k^{2}p_{k}<\infty;\\
F(u)&=1-u-\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}p_{k}(1-u)^{k},\ u\in[0,1].\end{split}

(PROB)

Note that such
F
always fulfills (
SC
), is smooth and strictly concave on
(0,1)
.

A fundamental result is the connection of the solution to (
F-KPP
) and the expected number of particles of the branching process. This is sometimes referred to as the McKean representation. The respective result in the homogeneous setting has been proven in
[
20
]
and
[
27
]
also.

Proposition 2.1
.

Let
w_{0}\in\mathcal{I}_{\text{F-KPP}}
,
\xi
be Hölder continuous and
F
fulfill (
PROB
). Then
\mathbb{P}
-a.s. the solution to (
F-KPP
) is given by

w(t,x)=1-\emph{\texttt{E}}_{x}^{\xi}\Big[\prod_{\nu\in N(t)}\big(1-w_{0}(X_{t}^{\nu})\big)\Big],\quad(t,x)\in[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}.

(McKean)

For the proof see Section
A.5
.

Remark 2.2
.

We will frequently use the application of the latter result to functions
w_{0}=\mathds{1}_{(-\infty,0]}
, resulting in

\displaystyle w(t,x)

\displaystyle=1-\texttt{E}_{x}^{\xi}\Big[\prod_{\nu\in N(t)}\mathds{1}_{(0,\infty)}(X_{t}^{\nu})\Big]=1-\texttt{P}_{x}^{\xi}\big(X_{t}^{\nu}>0\ \forall\nu\in N(t)\big)=\texttt{P}_{x}^{\xi}\big(N^{\leq}(t,0)\geq 1\big).

Furthermore, we will need the so-called
many-to-few
lemma, which breaks down the moments of the branching process to a functional of the Brownian paths. For our purposes, it suffices to state it up to the second moments (
many-to-one
and
many-to-two formula
).

Proposition 2.3
.

Let
(p_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
fulfill (
PROB
) and let
\varphi_{1}
,
\varphi_{2}:\,[0,\infty)\to[-\infty,\infty]
be càdlàg functions satisfying
\varphi_{1}\leq\varphi_{2}
. Then the first and second moments of the number of particles in
N(t)
whose genealogy stays between
\varphi_{1}
and
\varphi_{2}
in the time interval
[0,t]
are given by

\displaystyle\emph{\texttt{E}}_{x}^{\xi}

\displaystyle\Big[\big|\nu\in N(t):\varphi_{1}(s)\leq X_{s}^{\nu}\leq\varphi_{2}(s)\ \forall s\in[0,t]\big|\Big]

\displaystyle=E_{x}\Big[\exp\Big\{\int_{0}^{t}\xi(B_{r})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r\Big\};\varphi_{1}(s)\leq B_{s}\leq\varphi_{2}(s)\ \forall s\in[0,t]\Big]\quad

(FK-1)

and

\displaystyle\emph{\texttt{E}}_{x}^{\xi}\Big[

\displaystyle\big|\nu\in N(t):\varphi_{1}(s)\leq X_{s}^{\nu}\leq\varphi_{2}(s)\ \forall s\in[0,t]\big|^{2}\Big]

\displaystyle=E_{x}\Big[\exp\Big\{\int_{0}^{t}\xi(B_{r})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r\Big\};\varphi_{1}(s)\leq B_{s}\leq\varphi_{2}(s)\ \forall s\in[0,t]\Big]

\displaystyle\qquad+(m_{2}-2)\int_{0}^{t}E_{x}\Big[\exp\Big\{\int_{0}^{s}\xi(B_{r})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r\Big\}\,\xi(B_{s})\mathds{1}_{\{\varphi_{1}(r)\leq B_{r}\leq\varphi_{2}(r)\ \forall 0\leq r\leq s\}}\Big.

(FK-2)

\displaystyle\qquad\quad\times\Big.\Big(E_{y}\big[\exp\big\{\int_{0}^{t-s}\xi(B_{r})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r\big\}\,\mathds{1}_{\{\varphi_{1}(r+s)\leq B_{r}\leq\varphi_{2}(r+s)\ \forall 0\leq r\leq t-s\}}\big]\Big)^{2}_{|_{y=B_{s}}}\Big]\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s,

respectively.

The proof of the first identity follows from
[
19
, Section 4.1]
.
The second identity can be shown by using
[
19
, Lemma 1]
, conditioning on the first splitting of the so-called “spines”, similarly to the proof of
[
16
, (2.6)]
for
n=2
there. Indeed, one has to consider branching Brownian motion instead of branching random walk and replace binary branching by general branching. Then the expectation of the quantity in display
[
16
, (2.15)]
turns into the second summand in (
FK-2
), because the first splitting rate of the two “spine particles” at site
y
is
(m_{2}-2)\xi(y)
.

As a consequence of Proposition
2.3
, the solution to (
PAM
) can be expressed by a functional of the branching process, i.e. we have that the solution to (
PAM
) is given by

\displaystyle u(t,x)

\displaystyle=E_{x}\Big[\exp\Big\{\int_{0}^{t}\xi(B_{s})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s\Big\}\,u_{0}(B_{t})\Big]=\texttt{E}_{x}^{\xi}\Big[\sum_{\nu\in N(t)}u_{0}(X_{t}^{\nu})\Big].

As a special case for
u_{0}=\mathds{1}_{(-\infty,0]}
, this turns into

\displaystyle u(t,x)=\texttt{E}_{x}^{\xi}\big[N^{\leq}(t,0)\big]

\displaystyle=E_{x}\Big[\exp\Big\{\int_{0}^{t}\xi(B_{s})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s\Big\};B_{t}\leq 0\Big].

(2.2)

2.2
Change of measure

The first tool is a change of measure which makes it typical for the Brownian motion in the Feynman-Kac formula started at
tv,
some
v\neq 0,
to be close to the origin at time
t.

For this purpose, let
(\xi(x))_{x\in\mathbb{R}}
be as in (
BDD
) and define the shifted potential

\zeta:=\xi-\texttt{es}.

Then
\mathbb{P}
-a.s.,

\zeta(x)\in[-(\texttt{es}-\texttt{ei}),0]\quad\forall x\in\mathbb{R}.

(2.3)

We will oftentimes write

H_{y}:=\inf\big\{t\geq 0:B_{t}=y\big\},\quad y\in\mathbb{R},\quad\text{ and }\quad\tau_{i}:=H_{i-1}-H_{i},\quad i\in\mathbb{Z},

(2.4)

for the first hitting times and their pairwise differences.
The above shift of
\xi
gives rise to a change of measure
which will play a crucial role in the following. For
x,y\in\mathbb{R}
as well as
\eta\leq 0
define the probability measures
P_{x,y}^{\zeta,\eta}
via

\displaystyle P_{x,y}^{\zeta,\eta}(A)

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{Z_{x,y}^{\zeta,\eta}}E_{x}\Big[\exp\Big\{\int_{0}^{H_{x-y}}\big(\zeta(B_{s})+\eta\big)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s\Big\};A\Big],\quad A\in\sigma\big(B_{t\wedge H_{x-y}}:t\geq 0\big),

(2.5)

with normalizing constant

\displaystyle Z_{x,y}^{\zeta,\eta}

\displaystyle:=E_{x}\Big[\exp\Big\{\int_{0}^{H_{x-y}}\big(\zeta(B_{s})+\eta\big)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s\Big\}\Big]\in(0,\infty),

where
P_{x}
and
E_{x}
,
x\in\mathbb{R}
, are defined below (
1.10
).
For
A\in\sigma\big(B_{t\wedge H_{x-y}}:t\geq 0\big)
, using the strong Markov property at time
H_{x-y}
, we infer
P_{x,y}^{\zeta,\eta}(A)=P_{x,y^{\prime}}^{\zeta,\eta}(A)
for all
y^{\prime}\geq y
. Thus, by Kolmogorov’s extension theorem (see e.g.
[
35
, Theorem 2.4.3]
),

\big(P_{x,y}^{\zeta,\eta}\big)_{y\geq 0}\text{ can be extended to a unique probability measure }P_{x}^{\zeta,\eta}\text{ on }\sigma\big(B_{t}:t\geq 0\big).

(2.6)

We write
E_{x}^{\zeta,\eta}
for the corresponding expectation and introduce the logarithmic moment generating functions

\displaystyle L_{x}^{\zeta}(\eta)

\displaystyle:=\ln E_{x}\Big[\exp\Big\{\int_{0}^{H_{\lceil x\rceil-1}}\big(\zeta(B_{s})+\eta\big)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s\Big\}\Big],\quad x>0,

(2.7)

\displaystyle\overline{L}_{x}^{\zeta}(\eta)

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{x}\sum_{i=1}^{x}L_{i}^{\zeta}(\eta)=\frac{1}{x}\ln E_{x}\Big[\exp\Big\{\int_{0}^{H_{0}}(\zeta(B_{s})+\eta)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s\Big\}\Big],\quad x>0,

where we recall the notation introduced in Section
1.8
, and
where the last equality is due to the Markov property. In addition, set

L(\eta):=\mathbb{E}\big[L_{1}^{\zeta}(\eta)\big].

(2.8)

Due to (
2.3
),
for any
\eta\leq 0

the quantities above are well-defined, and it is easy to check that in this case and under (
BDD
), the expressions defined in (
2.7
)–(
2.8
) are finite. We have the following useful properties.

Lemma 2.4
.

(a)

The function
(-\infty,0)\ni\eta\mapsto L(\eta)
is infinitely differentiable and its derivative
L^{\prime}(\eta)
is positive and monotonically strictly increasing.

(b)

We have
\mathbb{P}\text{-a.s.}
that

\lim_{x\to\infty}\overline{L}_{x}^{\zeta}(\eta)=L(\eta)\quad\text{for all }\eta\leq 0.

(2.9)

(c)

L^{\prime}(\eta)\downarrow 0
as
\eta\downarrow-\infty

(d)

For every
v>v_{c}:=\frac{1}{L^{\prime}(0-)}

(where
\frac{1}{+\infty}:=0
), which we call
critical velocity
, there exists a

\text{ unique solution $\overline{\eta}(v)<0$ to the equation }L^{\prime}(\overline{\eta}(v))=\frac{1}{v}.

(2.10)

\overline{\eta}(v)
can be characterized as the unique maximizer to
(-\infty,0]\ni\eta\mapsto\frac{\eta}{v}-L(\eta)
, i.e.

\sup_{\eta\leq 0}\Big(\frac{\eta}{v}-L(\eta)\Big)=\frac{\overline{\eta}(v)}{v}-L\big(\overline{\eta}(v)\big).

(2.11)

The function
(v_{c},\infty)\ni v\mapsto\overline{\eta}(v)
is continuously differentiable and strictly decreasing.

Proof of Lemma
2.4
.

(a)

This follows from Lemma
A.1
.

(b)

By
[
14
, Theorem 7.5.1]
, for every
\eta\leq 0
we get
\mathbb{P}
-a.s. that

\lim_{x\to\infty}\overline{L}^{\zeta}_{x}(\eta)=\mathbb{E}\big[L_{1}^{\zeta}(\eta)\,|\,\mathcal{F}^{\zeta}_{\text{inv}}\big],

where
\mathcal{F}^{\zeta}_{\text{inv}}
is the
\sigma
-algebra of all
\mathbb{P}
-invariant sets. Due to our standing assumptions, the family
\zeta(x),

x\in\mathbb{R},
is mixing and thus ergodic. Thus,
\mathcal{F}^{\zeta}_{\text{inv}}
is
\mathbb{P}
-trivial, i.e.,
\mathbb{E}\big[L_{1}^{\zeta}(\eta)\,|\,\mathcal{F}^{\zeta}_{\text{inv}}\big]=L(\eta)
. By continuity of the functions
\overline{L}^{\zeta}_{x}
and
L
, the statement follows.

(c)

We note that
L
is strictly increasing and strictly convex on
(-\infty,0)
by (a) and

L(\eta)\geq\mathbb{E}\Big[\ln E_{1}\big[e^{-(\texttt{es}-\texttt{ei}-\eta)H_{0}}\big]\Big]=-\sqrt{2(\texttt{es}-\texttt{ei}-\eta)}\quad\text{for all }\eta\leq 0,

where the equality is due to
[
4
, (2.0.1), p. 204]
.
Thus, we infer that its derivative
L^{\prime}(\eta)
tends to
0
as
\eta\to-\infty
.

(d)

Using that
1/v_{c}>1/v
(where
\frac{1}{0}:=+\infty
) and the fact that
L^{\prime}
is strictly increasing with
L^{\prime}(\eta)\downarrow 0
for
\eta\downarrow-\infty
, we can find a unique
\overline{\eta}(v)<0
such that
L^{\prime}(\overline{\eta}(v))=1/v
, giving (
2.10
). On the other hand, (
2.11
) is a direct consequence of (
2.10
) and standard properties of the Legendre transformations of strictly convex functions.
Because
L^{\prime}
is strictly increasing and smooth on
(-\infty,0)
, it has a strictly increasing inverse function
(L^{\prime})^{-1}
, which is differentiable on
(0,1/v_{c})
. By (
2.10
), for
v>v_{c}
we have
\overline{\eta}(v)=(L^{\prime})^{-1}(1/v)
and thus by the formula for the derivative of the inverse function we get

\overline{\eta}^{\prime}(v)=-\frac{1}{v^{2}}\cdot\frac{1}{L^{\prime\prime}(\overline{\eta}(v))}.

Because the right-hand side of the latter display is continuous in
v
and negative, we conclude.

∎

We use the standard notation
L^{*}:\,\mathbb{R}\to(-\infty,\infty]
to denote the Legendre transformation

\displaystyle v

\displaystyle\mapsto\sup_{\eta\leq 0}\big(\eta v-L(\eta)\big)

of
L
.
Lemma
2.4
entails that

\displaystyle L^{*}(1/v)=\frac{\overline{\eta}(v)}{v}-L\big(\overline{\eta}(v)\big).

(2.12)

In the next part of this section, we are interested in a suitable
tilting parameter

\eta_{x}^{\zeta}(v)

such that

E_{x}^{\zeta,\eta_{x}^{\zeta}(v)}\big[H_{0}\big]=\frac{x}{v},\quad x>0,\ v>0,

(2.13)

holds true (setting
\eta_{x}^{\zeta}(v):=0
if no such parameter exists).
For
\eta_{x}^{\zeta}(v)
fulfilling (
2.13
) we observe that under

P_{x}^{\zeta,\eta_{x}^{\zeta}(v)}
,
the Brownian motion is tilted to have time-averaged velocity
v
until it reaches the origin. In the remaining part of this section, see Lemma
2.5
, we will show that for suitable
v
and
x
large enough, a tilting parameter as postulated in (
2.13
) actually exists. Furthermore, we will show that the random parameter
\eta_{x}^{\zeta}(v)
concentrates around the deterministic
\overline{\eta}(v)
defined in (
2.10
). The last result is a perturbation estimate for
\eta_{x}^{\zeta}(v)
in
x,
cf. Lemma
2.7
.

2.3
Concentration inequalities

We have the following result regarding the existence (or, more precisely, negativity) and concentration properties of the postulated parameter
\eta_{x}^{\zeta}(v)
.

Lemma 2.5
.

(a)

For every
v>v_{c}
there exists a finite random variable
\mathcal{N}=\mathcal{N}(v)
such that for all
x\geq\mathcal{N}
the solution
\eta_{x}^{\zeta}(v)<0
to (
2.13
) exists.

(b)

For each
q\in\mathbb{N}
and each compact interval
V\subset(v_{c},\infty)
, there exists
:=\Cr{const:concEtaEmpLT}(V,q)\in(0,\infty)
such that

\mathbb{P}\Big(\sup_{v\in V}\sup_{x\in[n,n+1)}|\eta_{x}^{\zeta}(v)-\overline{\eta}(v)|\geq\Cr{const:concEtaEmpLT}\sqrt{\frac{\ln n}{n}}\Big)\leq\Cr{const:concEtaEmpLT}n^{-q}\qquad\text{for all }n\in\mathbb{N}.

(2.14)

Proof.

We recall that due to
Lemma
A.1
, the tilting parameter
\eta_{x}^{\zeta}(v)
can alternatively be characterized as the unique solution
\eta_{x}^{\zeta}(v)\in(-\infty,0)
to

\big(\overline{L}^{\zeta}_{x}\big)^{\prime}(\eta_{x}^{\zeta}(v))=\frac{1}{v},

(2.15)

if the solution exists, and
\eta_{x}^{\zeta}(v)=0
otherwise.
We start with noting that Part
(a)
directly follows from Part
(b)
. Indeed, let
A_{n}
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
, be the event in the probability on the left-hand side of (
2.14
). Then
\sum_{n}\mathbb{P}(A_{n})<\infty
for
q\geq 2
. By the first Borel-Cantelli lemma,
\mathbb{P}
-a.s. only finitely many of the
A_{n}
occur. In combination with
the fact that
\overline{\eta}(v)<0
, cf. (
2.10
), this implies that
\mathbb{P}
-a.s., the value of
\eta_{x}^{\zeta}(v)
can only vanish for
x>0
small enough. In particular, we deduce the existence of a
\mathbb{P}
-a.s. finite random variable
\mathcal{N}
as postulated.

Hence, it remains to show (
2.14
).
For this purpose, in the following lemma we investigate the fluctuations of the functions through which the parameters

\eta_{x}^{\zeta}(v)
and
\overline{\eta}(v)
are implicitly defined; we will then infer the desired bounds on the fluctuations of the parameters themselves through perturbation estimates for these functions.

Lemma 2.6
.

For every compact interval
\triangle\subset(-\infty,0)
and each
q\in\mathbb{N},
there exists a constant
=\Cr{const:concEmpLT}(\triangle,q)\in(0,\infty)
such that

\mathbb{P}\Big(\sup_{\eta\in\triangle}\sup_{x\in[n,n+1)}\Big|\big(\overline{L}_{x}^{\zeta}\big)^{\prime}(\eta)-L^{\prime}(\eta)\Big|\geq\Cr{const:concEmpLT}\sqrt{\frac{\ln n}{n}}\Big)\leq\Cr{const:concEmpLT}n^{-q}\qquad\text{for all }n\in\mathbb{N}.

(2.16)

In order not to hinder the flow of reading, we
postpone the proof of this auxiliary result to the end of the proof of Lemma
2.5
and finish the proof of Lemma
2.5
(b) first. Let
q\in\mathbb{N}
and
V\subset(v_{c},\infty)
be a compact interval. By Lemma
A.1
, for each compact
\triangle\subset(-\infty,0)
we have
\mathbb{P}
-a.s.,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Cr{constDerLMG}^{-1}\leq\inf_{\eta\in\triangle}L^{\prime\prime}(\eta)&\leq\sup_{\eta\in\triangle}L^{\prime\prime}(\eta)\leq\Cr{constDerLMG},\\
\Cr{constDerLMG}^{-1}\leq\inf_{x\geq 1}\,\inf_{\eta\in\triangle}\big(\overline{L}_{x}^{\zeta}\big)^{\prime\prime}(\eta)&\leq\sup_{x\geq 1}\,\sup_{\eta\in\triangle}\big(\overline{L}_{x}^{\zeta}\big)^{\prime\prime}(\eta)\leq\Cr{constDerLMG}.\end{split}

(2.17)

Therefore, and because the function
V\ni v\mapsto\overline{\eta}(v)
is strictly decreasing by Lemma
2.4
, it is possible to find
N=N(V)\in\mathbb{N}
and a compact interval
\triangle=\triangle(N,V)\subset(-\infty,0),
where for notational convenience we write

V=[v_{*},v^{*}]\quad\text{ and }\quad\triangle=[\eta_{*},\eta^{*}],

(2.18)

such that, using standard calculus for sets,

\big(\overline{\eta}(V)-\Cr{const:concEmpLT}\Cr{constDerLMG}\sqrt{\ln n/n}\big)\cup\big(\overline{\eta}(V)+\Cr{const:concEmpLT}\Cr{constDerLMG}\sqrt{\ln n/n}\big)\subset\triangle\quad\text{ for all }n\geq N.

Let
n\geq N
and assume that the complement of the event on the left-hand side of (
2.16
),

\sup_{\eta\in\triangle}\,\sup_{x\in[n,n+1)}\big|\big(\overline{L}_{x}^{\zeta}\big)^{\prime}(\eta)-L^{\prime}(\eta)\big|<\Cr{const:concEmpLT}\sqrt{\frac{\ln n}{n}},

(2.19)

occurs.
On this event, for all
v\in V
and all
x\in[n,n+1),

\big(\overline{L}_{x}^{\zeta}\big)^{\prime}\big(\overline{\eta}(v)-\Cr{const:concEmpLT}\Cr{constDerLMG}\sqrt{\ln n/n}\big)\leq\frac{1}{v}\leq\big(\overline{L}_{x}^{\zeta}\big)^{\prime}\big(\overline{\eta}(v)+\Cr{const:concEmpLT}\Cr{constDerLMG}\sqrt{\ln n/n}\big)

(2.20)

and thus, due to the strict monotonicity of
(\overline{L}_{x}^{\zeta})^{\prime}
implied by (
2.17
), there exists a unique
\eta_{x}^{\zeta}(v)\in\triangle
such that
\big(\overline{L}_{x}^{\zeta}\big)^{\prime}(\eta_{x}^{\zeta}(v))=1/v
. Due to (
2.20
), still assuming (
2.19
), we have

\sup_{v\in V}\,\sup_{x\in[n,n+1)}|\overline{\eta}(v)-\eta_{x}^{\zeta}(v)|\leq\Cr{const:concEmpLT}\Cr{constDerLMG}\sqrt{\frac{\ln n}{n}}.

Thus, for
n\geq N
, choosing
\Cr{const:concEtaEmpLT}>2\Cr{const:concEmpLT}\Cr{constDerLMG}
, the probability in (
2.14
) is bounded by the right-hand side of (
2.16
), which finishes the proof.
∎

It remains to prove Lemma
2.6
.

Proof of Lemma
2.6
.

Applying the strong Markov property, we get

x\big(\overline{L}_{x}^{\zeta}\big)^{\prime}(\eta)=E_{x}^{\zeta,\eta}(H_{0})=E_{x}^{\zeta,\eta}\big[H_{\lfloor x\rfloor}\big]+E_{\lfloor x\rfloor}^{\zeta,\eta}\big[H_{0}\big]=E_{x}^{\zeta,\eta}\big[H_{\lfloor x\rfloor}\big]+\lfloor x\rfloor\big(\overline{L}_{\lfloor x\rfloor}^{\zeta}\big)^{\prime}(\eta).

Furthermore,

0\leq E_{x}^{\zeta,\eta}\big[H_{\lfloor x\rfloor}\big]=\big(L_{x}^{\zeta}\big)^{\prime}(\eta)\leq\Cr{constDerLMG}
by (
A.1
) and Lemma
A.1
b), and thus also
0\leq\big(\overline{L}_{x}^{\zeta}\big)^{\prime}(\eta)=\frac{1}{x}\sum_{i=1}^{x}\big(L_{i}^{\zeta}\big)^{\prime}(\eta)\leq\Cr{constDerLMG}
for all
x\geq 1
and all
\eta\in\triangle
,
\mathbb{P}
-a.s. As a consequence, we get that for all
x\geq 1,

\big|\big(\overline{L}_{x}^{\zeta}\big)^{\prime}(\eta)-L^{\prime}(\eta)\big|\leq\big|\big(\overline{L}_{\lfloor x\rfloor}^{\zeta}\big)^{\prime}(\eta)-L^{\prime}(\eta)\big|+\frac{2\Cr{constDerLMG}}{\lfloor x\rfloor}.

It is therefore enough to prove

\mathbb{P}\Big(\sup_{\eta\in\triangle}\Big|\big(\overline{L}_{n}^{\zeta}\big)^{\prime}(\eta)-L^{\prime}(\eta)\Big|\geq\Cr{const:concEmpLT}\sqrt{\frac{\ln n}{n}}\Big)\leq\Cr{const:concEmpLT}n^{-q}\qquad\text{for all }n\in\mathbb{N}.

(2.21)

For each
\eta\in\triangle
,
((L_{i}^{\zeta})^{\prime}(\eta)-L^{\prime}(\eta))_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}
is a family of stationary, centered and bounded random variables. Furthermore, they fulfill the exponential mixing condition (
A.11
) due to Lemma
A.2
. Since
\sigma((L_{i}^{\zeta})^{\prime}(\eta):i\geq k)\subset\sigma(\xi(x):x\geq k-1)
and (
•
‣
1.4
), setting
Y_{i}:=(L_{i}^{\zeta})^{\prime}(\eta)-L^{\prime}(\eta)
, the left-hand side in (
A.15
) is bounded by some constant
c_{2}>0
, uniformly for every
i
. Then setting
m_{i}:=c_{1}
, condition (
A.15
) is fulfilled and we can apply the Hoeffding-type inequality from Corollary
A.5
to get
c_{2}>0
such that

\mathbb{P}\Big(\Big|\big(\overline{L}_{n}^{\zeta}\big)^{\prime}(\eta)-L^{\prime}(\eta)\Big|\geq c_{2}\sqrt{\frac{\ln n}{n}}\Big)\leq c_{2}n^{-q-1}\qquad\text{for all }\eta\in\triangle\text{ and all }n\in\mathbb{N}.

Let
\triangle_{n}:=(\triangle\cap\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{Z})\cup\{\eta_{*},\eta^{*}\}
, recalling the notation of (
2.18
). Because
|\triangle\cap\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{Z}|\leq n\cdot\text{diam}(\triangle)+1
, taking advantage of the previous display we infer

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Big(\sup_{\eta\in\triangle_{n}}

\displaystyle\Big|\big(\overline{L}_{n}^{\zeta}\big)^{\prime}(\eta)-L^{\prime}(\eta)\Big|\geq c_{2}\sqrt{\frac{\ln n}{n}}\Big)

\displaystyle\leq|\triangle_{n}|\cdot\sup_{\eta\in\triangle_{n}}\mathbb{P}\Big(\Big|\big(\overline{L}_{n}^{\zeta}\big)^{\prime}(\eta)-L^{\prime}(\eta)\Big|\geq c_{2}\sqrt{\frac{\ln n}{n}}\Big)\leq c_{3}(\triangle)n^{-q},\qquad\text{for all }n\in\mathbb{N}.

By Lemma
A.1
b), we have
\mathbb{P}
-a.s.
\sup_{n}\sup_{\eta\in\triangle}\big|\big(\overline{L}_{n}^{\zeta}(\eta)\big)^{\prime\prime}\big|\vee\big|L^{\prime\prime}(\eta)\big|\leq\Cr{constDerLMG}
. Thus, the mean value theorem entails that

\displaystyle\sup_{\eta\in\triangle}\big|\big(\overline{L}_{n}^{\zeta}\big)^{\prime}(\eta)-L^{\prime}(\eta)\big|

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{\eta\in\triangle_{n}}\big|\big(\overline{L}_{n}^{\zeta}\big)^{\prime}(\eta)-L^{\prime}(\eta)\big|+\frac{2\Cr{constDerLMG}}{n},

and thus we find
\Cr{const:concEmpLT}>0
such that (
2.21
) and hence (
2.16
) hold true.
∎

In what comes below, many results will implicitly depend on the choice of compact intervals
V
and
\triangle,
which have already occurred before.
Thus, in order to avoid ambiguity and due to assumption (
VEL
), we will now

\begin{gathered}\text{fix arbitrary compact intervals $V\subset(v_{c},\infty)$ and $\triangle=\triangle(V)\subset(-\infty,0)$ such that }\\
\text{$v_{0}\in\text{int}(V)$ and $\overline{\eta}(V)\subset\text{int}(\triangle)$}.\end{gathered}

(2.22)

Furthermore, due to Lemma
2.5
, there exists a
\mathbb{P}
-a.s. finite random variable
=\Cr{constHn}(\xi,\Cr{const:concEtaEmpLT}(V,2))
such that

\mathcal{H}_{x}:=\mathcal{H}_{x}(V):=\big\{\eta_{x}^{\zeta}(v)\in\triangle\text{ for all }v\in V\big\}\quad\text{ occurs for all }x\geq\Cr{constHn}.

(2.23)

We write

\big(\overline{L}_{x}^{\zeta}\big)^{*}\Big(\frac{1}{v}\Big)=\sup_{\eta<0}\Big(\frac{\eta}{v}-\overline{L}^{\zeta}_{x}(\eta)\Big)=\frac{\eta_{x}^{\zeta}(v)}{v}-\overline{L}^{\zeta}_{x}(\eta_{x}^{\zeta}(v)),\quad x\geq 1,

for Legendre transformation
of the weighted averages.
We also recall to set
\eta_{x}^{\zeta}(v)=0
, if there is no solution
\eta_{x}^{\zeta}(v)\in\triangle
to (
2.15
); note that this can only happen on
\mathcal{H}_{x}^{c}
.

In order to show an invariance principle for the Legendre transformation
(\overline{L}_{x}^{\zeta})^{*}
in the following section,
we now derive a perturbation result on the tilting parameter
\eta_{x}^{\zeta}(v)
in
x.

Lemma 2.7
.

There exists a constant
>0
such that
\mathbb{P}
-a.s., for all
x\in(0,\infty)
large enough, uniformly in
v\in V
and
0\leq h\leq x
,

\big|\eta_{x}^{\zeta}(v)-\eta_{x+h}^{\zeta}(v)|\leq\Cr{const_{e}ta_{p}ert}\frac{h}{x}.

(2.24)

Proof.

By Lemma
2.5
we can choose
x
large enough such that
\eta_{y}^{\zeta}(v)\in\triangle
for all
y\geq x
and all
v\in V
. For
h=0
, the statement is obvious. For
0<h\leq x
, it suffices to show that there exists
c_{1}>0
such that

\sup_{\eta\in\triangle}\big|\big(\overline{L}_{x+h}^{\zeta}\big)^{\prime}(\eta)-\big(\overline{L}_{x}^{\zeta}\big)^{\prime}(\eta)\big|\leq c_{1}\frac{h}{x}.

(2.25)

Indeed, using (
2.15
) we can write

\displaystyle\big(\overline{L}_{x+h}^{\zeta}\big)^{\prime}(\eta_{x+h}^{\zeta}(v))-\big(\overline{L}_{x}^{\zeta}\big)^{\prime}(\eta_{x+h}^{\zeta}(v))

\displaystyle=\big(\overline{L}_{x}^{\zeta}\big)^{\prime}(\eta_{x}^{\zeta}(v))-\big(\overline{L}_{x}^{\zeta}\big)^{\prime}(\eta_{x+h}^{\zeta}(v))

\displaystyle=\big(\overline{L}_{x}^{\zeta}\big)^{\prime\prime}(\widetilde{\eta})(\eta_{x}^{\zeta}(v)-\eta_{x+h}^{\zeta}(v))

for some
\widetilde{\eta}\in\triangle
between
\eta_{x}^{\zeta}(v)
and
\eta_{x+h}^{\zeta}(v)
. By the second display in (
2.17
) we know that
\mathbb{P}
-a.s.
\inf_{\eta\in\triangle,x\geq 1}(\overline{L}_{x}^{\zeta}\big)^{\prime\prime}(\eta)\geq\Cr{constDerLMG}^{-1}
. Using this, inequality (
2.24
) is a direct consequence of (
2.25
) with
\Cr{const_{e}ta_{p}ert}:=c_{1}\Cr{constDerLMG}
.
To prove (
2.25
), recall that for all
\eta\in\triangle
,
x\geq 1,
and
0<h\leq x,
by the strong Markov property applied at time
H_{x},

\displaystyle\big(\overline{L}_{x+h}^{\zeta}\big)^{\prime}(\eta)-\big(\overline{L}_{x}^{\zeta}\big)^{\prime}(\eta)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{x+h}\big(E_{x+h}^{\zeta,\eta}\big[H_{x}\big]+E_{x}^{\zeta,\eta}[H_{0}]\big)-\frac{1}{x}E_{x}^{\zeta,\eta}[H_{0}]

\displaystyle=-\frac{h}{x+h}\big(\overline{L}_{x}^{\zeta}\big)^{\prime}(\eta)+\frac{h}{x+h}\frac{1}{h}E_{x+h}^{\zeta,\eta}[H_{x}].

Finally, recall that by Lemma
A.1
there exists
\Cr{constDerLMG}=\Cr{constDerLMG}(\triangle)>0
such that
\mathbb{P}
-a.s. we have
\sup_{\eta\in\triangle,x>0}\big|\big(\overline{L}_{x}^{\zeta}\big)^{\prime}(\eta)\big|\leq\Cr{constDerLMG}
. By exactly the same argument used for the proof of the latter inequality (see proof of (
A.1
)), one can show that also
\sup_{\eta\in\triangle,x,h>0}\big|\frac{1}{h}E_{x+h}^{\zeta,\eta}[H_{x}]\big|\leq\Cr{constDerLMG}
holds
\mathbb{P}
-a.s. with the same constant
\Cr{constDerLMG}
. (
2.25
) now follows choosing
c_{1}:=2\Cr{constDerLMG}
.
∎

3
Large deviations and perturbation results for the PAM

The main objective of this section is to establish certain exact large deviation results for hitting times of Brownian motion under the tilted measures introduced above, and then to apply these in order to obtain perturbation results.
For this purpose let

\displaystyle\begin{split}V_{x}^{\zeta,v}(\eta)&:=\frac{\eta}{v}-L_{x}^{\zeta}(\eta),\quad x\in\mathbb{R},\\
\sigma^{2}_{v}&:=\text{Var}_{\mathbb{P}}(V_{1}^{\zeta,v}(\overline{\eta}(v))+2\sum_{i=2}^{\infty}\text{Cov}_{\mathbb{P}}\big(V_{1}^{\zeta,v}(\overline{\eta}(v)),V_{i}^{\zeta,v}(\overline{\eta}(v)\big),\quad\sigma_{v}:=\sqrt{\sigma^{2}_{v}},\quad v\in V.\end{split}

(3.1)

We start with observing that
\sigma_{v}^{2}\in[0,\infty)
for all
v\in V
. Indeed,
(\widetilde{L}_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
, where
\widetilde{L}_{i}:=L_{i}^{\zeta}(\overline{\eta}(v))-\mathbb{E}[L_{i}^{\zeta}(\overline{\eta}(v))]
, is a sequence of bounded (see Lemma
A.1
), centered and mixing (see Lemma
A.2
) random variables, giving

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\big|\text{Cov}_{\mathbb{P}}\big(V_{1}^{\zeta,v}(\overline{\eta}(v)),V_{i}^{\zeta,v}(\overline{\eta}(v))\big)\big|

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\big|\mathbb{E}\big[\widetilde{L}_{1}\widetilde{L}_{i}\big]\big|=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\big|\mathbb{E}\big[\widetilde{L}_{i}\mathbb{E}[\widetilde{L}_{1}\,|\,\mathcal{F}^{i-1}]\big]\big|<\infty,

(3.2)

where the last inequality is due to uniform boundedness of
\widetilde{L}_{i}
in
i
, (
A.9
) and the summability criterion in (
•
‣
1.4
). Thus
\sigma_{v}^{2}<\infty
. Furthermore,
\sigma_{v}^{2}\geq 0
is due to (
3.2
) and
[
33
, Lemma 1.1]
.

We now introduce the process
W^{v}_{x}(t)
of empirical Legendre transformations

W^{v}_{x}(t):={t\sqrt{x}}\Big((\overline{L}_{xt}^{\zeta})^{*}(1/v)-L^{*}(1/v)\Big),\quad t,x>0,\ v\in V,

(3.3)

and set
W_{0}^{v}(t)=W_{x}^{v}(0)=0
for
t,x>0
,
v\in V
, and obtain a first functional Central limit theorem for it.

Proposition 3.1
.

For every
v\in V
,
W_{n}^{v}(1)
converges in
\mathbb{P}
-distribution to a centered Gaussian random variable with variance
\sigma_{v}^{2}\geq 0
. If
\sigma_{v}^{2}>0
, the sequence of processes

[0,\infty)\ni t\mapsto\frac{1}{\sigma_{v}}W_{n}^{v}(t),\quad n\in\mathbb{N},

converges in
\mathbb{P}
-distribution to a standard Brownian motion in the sense of weak convergence of measures on
C([0,\infty))
with topology induced by the metric
\rho
from (
1.12
).

Proof.

It is sufficient to show the claim if

(W^{v}_{n}(t))_{t\in[0,\infty)}
is replaced by
(W_{n}^{v}(t)\cdot\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{nt}})_{t\in[0,\infty)},

n\in\mathbb{N},
with
\mathcal{H}_{nt}
as defined in (
2.23
), since the
\mathbb{P}
-probability of
\mathcal{H}_{nt}
tends to
1
for
n\to\infty
by Lemma
2.5
. In the notation of (
3.1
), setting

S_{x}^{\zeta,v}(\eta):=\sum_{i=1}^{x}V_{i}^{\zeta,v}(\eta),\ x\in\mathbb{R},

(3.4)

on
\mathcal{H}_{nt}
we have

\displaystyle\big(\overline{L}_{nt}^{\zeta}\big)^{*}\Big(\frac{1}{v}\Big)

\displaystyle=\frac{\eta_{nt}^{\zeta}(v)}{v}-\overline{L}^{\zeta}_{nt}(\eta_{nt}^{\zeta}(v))=\frac{1}{{nt}}\sum_{i=1}^{nt}V_{i}^{\zeta,v}(\eta_{nt}^{\zeta}(v))=\frac{1}{{nt}}S_{nt}^{\zeta,v}(\eta_{nt}^{\zeta}(v)).

Thus, we can rewrite the relevant term as a sum of three differences

\displaystyle\begin{split}{nt}\Big((\overline{L}_{nt}^{\zeta})^{*}(1/v)-L^{*}(1/v)\Big)&=\Big(S_{nt}^{\zeta,v}(\eta_{nt}^{\zeta}(v))-S_{nt}^{\zeta,v}(\overline{\eta}(v))\Big)+\Big(S_{nt}^{\zeta,v}(\overline{\eta}(v))-\mathbb{E}\big[S_{nt}^{\zeta,v}(\overline{\eta}(v))\big]\Big)\\
&\quad+\Big(\mathbb{E}\big[S_{nt}^{\zeta,v}(\overline{\eta}(v))\big]-{nt}L^{*}(1/v)\Big),\end{split}

(3.5)

where we note that the third summand vanishes. Indeed, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[S_{nt}^{\zeta,v}(\overline{\eta}(v))\big]

\displaystyle=nt\Big(\frac{\overline{\eta}(v)}{v}-\mathbb{E}[L_{1}^{\zeta}(\overline{\eta}(v))]\Big)=ntL^{*}(1/v),

where the last equality is due to (
2.11
) and the definition of the Legendre transform. The proof is completed by the use of Lemmas
3.3
and
3.2
below, which show that the second summand of (
3.5
) exhibits the postulated diffusive behavior whereas the latter summand is negligible in that scaling.
∎

Lemma 3.2
.

For every
v\in V
,
\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\Big(S_{nt}^{\zeta,v}(\overline{\eta}(v))-\mathbb{E}\big[S_{nt}^{\zeta,v}(\overline{\eta}(v))\big]\Big)
converges in
\mathbb{P}
-distribution to a centered Gaussian random variable with variance
\sigma_{v}^{2}\geq 0
. If
\sigma_{v}^{2}>0
, the sequence of processes

[0,\infty)\ni t\mapsto\frac{1}{\sigma_{v}\sqrt{n}}\Big(S_{nt}^{\zeta,v}(\overline{\eta}(v))-\mathbb{E}\big[S_{nt}^{\zeta,v}(\overline{\eta}(v))\big]\Big),\quad n\in\mathbb{N},

converges in
\mathbb{P}
-distribution to a standard Brownian motion in the sense of weak convergence of measures on
C([0,\infty))
with topology induced by the metric
\rho
from (
1.12
).

Proof.

Let
\widetilde{L}_{i}:=L_{i}^{\zeta}(\overline{\eta}(v))-\mathbb{E}[L_{i}^{\zeta}(\overline{\eta}(v))]
,
\widetilde{V}_{i}:=V_{i}^{\zeta}(\overline{\eta}(v))-\mathbb{E}[V_{i}^{\zeta}(\overline{\eta}(v))]
and
M\in\mathbb{N}
. Further set
\widetilde{L}_{i}^{(M)}:=\sum_{j=1+(i-1)M}^{iM}\widetilde{L}_{j}
. Then
(\widetilde{L}_{i}^{(M)})_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}
is a sequence of centered, stationary and (by Lemma
A.1
) bounded random variables. To show the central limit theorem on
C([0,M])
, we will use the method of martingale approximation from
[
17
]
, which is summarized as a theorem by Nolen in
[
28
, section 2.3]
and turns out to be applicable in our situation. That is, we have to make sure that condition
[
28
, (2.36)]
is fulfilled. Indeed, replacing in (
A.13
)
\mathcal{F}^{j}
by
\mathcal{F}_{k}
and noting that quantity
A
in (
A.13
) is
\mathcal{F}_{k}
-measurable we get

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}|\widetilde{L}_{0}^{(M)}-\mathbb{E}[\widetilde{L}_{0}^{(M)}\,|\,\mathcal{F}_{k}]|

\displaystyle\leq c_{1}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}e^{-k/c_{1}}<\infty,

giving the convergence of the first series in
[
28
, (2.36)]
. Furthermore, using that
\widetilde{L}_{k}^{(M)}
is
\mathcal{F}^{(k-1)M}
-measurable and bounded, also recalling (
•
‣
1.4
), we get

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}|\mathbb{E}[\widetilde{L}_{k}^{(M)}\,|\,\mathcal{F}_{0}]|\leq\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\psi(k-1)\mathbb{E}[|\widetilde{L}_{0}^{(M)}|]<\infty.

Because the series in (
3.1
) is absolutely convergent, by
[
33
, Lemma 1.1]
and
[
28
, (2.37)]
we have
\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{E}\big[\big(\sum_{k=1}^{n}\widetilde{L}_{k}^{(M)}\big)^{2}\big]=M\cdot\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{Mn}\mathbb{E}\big[\big(\sum_{k=1}^{Mn}\widetilde{L}_{k}\big)^{2}\big]=M\cdot\sigma_{v}^{2}\in[0,\infty)
. Furthermore, if
\sigma_{v}^{2}>0
,
[
28
, Theorem 2.1]
entail that the sequence of processes

[0,1]\ni t\mapsto X_{n}^{(M)}(t):=\frac{1}{\sigma_{v}\sqrt{nM}}\big(\sum_{k=1}^{\lfloor nt\rfloor}\widetilde{L}_{k}^{(M)}+(nt-\lfloor nt\rfloor)\widetilde{L}_{\lfloor nt\rfloor+1}^{(M)}\big),\quad n\in\mathbb{N},

converge in
\mathbb{P}
-distribution to a standard Brownian motion
(B_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}
in the sense of weak convergence of measures on
C([0,1])
with the topology induced by the uniform metric.
Then by definition, above convergence also holds true for
(\widetilde{V}_{i})_{i\geq 1}
instead of
(\widetilde{L}_{i})_{i\geq 1}
. Furthermore, we have the uniform bound

\sup_{t\in[0,M],n\in\mathbb{N}}\Big|S_{nt}^{\zeta,v}(\overline{\eta}(v))-\big(\sum_{i=1}^{\lfloor nt/M\rfloor M}V_{i}^{\zeta,v}+(nt-\lfloor nt/M\rfloor M)\sum_{i=1+\lfloor nt/M\rfloor M}^{M+\lfloor nt/M\rfloor M}V^{\zeta,v}_{i}\big)\Big|\leq c_{2}\quad\mathbb{P}\text{-a.s.}

Consequently, the sequence
[0,M]\ni t\mapsto\frac{1}{\sigma_{v}\sqrt{n}}\Big(S_{nt}^{\zeta,v}(\overline{\eta}(v))-\mathbb{E}\big[S_{nt}^{\zeta,v}(\overline{\eta}(v))\big]\Big)
has the same weak limit as
\big(\sqrt{M}\cdot X_{n}^{(M)}(t/M)\big)_{t\in[0,M]}
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
, which converges to
\big(\sqrt{M}\cdot B(t/M)\big)_{t\in[0,M]}
and the latter process is a standard Brownian motion on
[0,M]
. Because
M\in\mathbb{N}
was arbitrary,
[
37
, first Theorem 2.4 on page 15]
gives weak convergence on
C([0,\infty))
.
∎

To show that the first summand in (
3.5
) is asymptotically negligible, we use the following result.

Lemma 3.3
.

There exists a constant
\in(0,\infty)
such that for every
v\in V
and
M>0
,

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{\ln n}\sup_{0\leq t\leq M}\Big|S_{nt}^{\zeta,v}(\eta_{nt}^{\zeta}(v))-S_{nt}^{\zeta,v}(\overline{\eta}(v))\Big|\leq\Cr{const_{c}oncSn}\quad\mathbb{P}\text{-a.s.}

Proof.

There exists a
\mathbb{P}
-a.s. finite time
\Cr{constHn}(\omega)
, defined before (
2.23
), such that for all
x\geq\Cr{constHn}
and all
v\in V
we have
\eta_{x}^{\zeta}(v)\in\triangle
. Furthermore, by Lemma
A.1
,

S_{x}^{\zeta,v}
is infinitely differentiable on
(-\infty,0)
, so for all
x\geq\Cr{constHn}
there exists
\widetilde{\eta}_{x}^{\zeta}(v)\in[\overline{\eta}(v)\wedge\eta_{x}^{\zeta}(v),\overline{\eta}(v)\vee\eta_{x}^{\zeta}(v)]
such that

\displaystyle S_{x}^{\zeta,v}(\overline{\eta}(v))

\displaystyle=S_{x}^{\zeta,v}(\eta_{x}^{\zeta}(v))+\big(S_{x}^{\zeta,v}\big)^{\prime}(\eta_{x}^{\zeta}(v))\big(\overline{\eta}(v)-\eta_{x}^{\zeta}(v)\big)+\frac{\big(S_{x}^{\zeta,v}\big)^{\prime\prime}(\widetilde{\eta}_{x}^{\zeta}(v))}{2}\big(\overline{\eta}(v)-\eta_{x}^{\zeta}(v)\big)^{2}.

Due to (
2.15
),
(S_{x}^{\zeta,v})^{\prime}(\eta_{x}^{\zeta}(v))=0
and by Lemma
A.1
we have
\sup_{\eta\in\triangle}\sup_{x\geq 1}\big|(S_{x}^{\zeta,v})^{\prime\prime}(\eta)\big|/x\leq c_{1}
.
By (
2.14
) and the first Borel-Cantelli lemma, there exists a finite random variable
\mathcal{N}_{2}\geq\Cr{constHn}
such that for
x\geq\mathcal{N}_{2}
the complementary event on the left-hand side of (
2.14
) occurs, hence

\sup_{x\geq\Cr{constHn}}\big(\eta_{x}^{\zeta}(v)-\overline{\eta}(v)\big)^{2}\frac{x}{\ln x}\leq\Cr{const:concEtaEmpLT}^{2}(V,2)

and thus

\displaystyle\sup_{x\geq\Cr{constHn}}\frac{1}{\ln x}\big|S_{x}^{\zeta,v}(\eta_{x}^{\zeta}(v))-S_{x}^{\zeta,v}(\overline{\eta}(v))\big|

\displaystyle\leq\Cr{const_{c}oncSn}

(3.6)

with
\Cr{const_{c}oncSn}:=\frac{c_{1}\Cr{const:concEtaEmpLT}^{2}(V,2)}{2}
. Finally, we have

\displaystyle\sup_{0\leq t\leq M}\Big|S_{nt}^{\zeta,v}(\eta_{nt}^{\zeta}(v))-S_{nt}^{\zeta,v}(\overline{\eta}(v))\Big|

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{0\leq x\leq\Cr{constHn}}\Big|S_{x}^{\zeta,v}(\eta_{x}^{\zeta}(v))-S_{x}^{\zeta,v}(\overline{\eta}(v))\Big|

\displaystyle\qquad+\sup_{\Cr{constHn}/n\leq t\leq M}\Big|S_{nt}^{\zeta,v}(\eta_{nt}^{\zeta}(v))-S_{nt}^{\zeta,v}(\overline{\eta}(v))\Big|

\displaystyle\leq 2\Cr{constHn}c_{2}+\Cr{const_{c}oncSn}\ln M+\Cr{const_{c}oncSn}\ln n,

where in the last inequality we used that
\mathbb{P}
-a.s., every summand in the definition of
S_{n}^{\zeta,\eta}
is uniformly bounded by
c_{2}
. The
\mathbb{P}
-a.s. finiteness of
\Cr{constHn}
gives the claim.
∎

As a by-product of the proof above we get an approximation result of
W_{x}^{v}
be a centered stationary sequence.

Corollary 3.4
.

For every
v\in V
and all
t
such that
vt\geq\Cr{constHn}
we have

\Big|\sqrt{vt}W_{vt}^{v}(1)-\sum_{i=1}^{vt}\big(L(\overline{\eta}(v))-L_{i}^{\zeta}(\overline{\eta}(v))\big)\Big|\leq\Cr{const_{c}oncSn}\ln v+\Cr{const_{c}oncSn}\ln t.

Proof.

By the definition of
W^{v}_{x}(t)
and
S_{x}^{\zeta,v}(\eta)
from (
3.3
) and (
3.4
), as well as the definition in (
2.12
) for the corresponding Legendre transformations, we have

\displaystyle\sqrt{vt}W_{vt}^{v}(1)

\displaystyle=vt\Big(\big(\overline{L}_{vt}^{\zeta}\big)^{*}(1/v)-L^{*}(1/v)\Big)=vt\Big(\frac{\eta_{vt}^{\zeta}(v)}{v}-\overline{L}_{vt}\big(\eta_{vt}^{\zeta}(v)\big)-\frac{\overline{\eta}(v)}{v}+L(\overline{\eta}(v))\Big)

\displaystyle=S_{vt}^{\zeta,v}\big(\eta_{vt}^{\zeta}(v)\big)-\sum_{i=1}^{vt}\big(\frac{\overline{\eta}(v)}{v}-L_{i}^{\zeta}(\overline{\eta}(v))\big)+\sum_{i=1}^{vt}\big(L(\overline{\eta}(v))-L_{i}^{\zeta}(\overline{\eta}(v))\big)

\displaystyle=S_{vt}^{\zeta,v}\big(\eta_{vt}^{\zeta}(v)\big)-S_{vt}^{\zeta,v}\big(\overline{\eta}(v)\big)+\sum_{i=1}^{vt}\big(L(\overline{\eta}(v))-L_{i}^{\zeta}(\overline{\eta}(v))\big).

Then we can conclude using (
3.6
).
∎

3.1
An exact large deviation result for auxiliary processes

For
x\geq 0
and
v>0
we introduce

\displaystyle Y_{v}^{\approx}(x)

\displaystyle:=E_{x}\Big[e^{\int_{0}^{H_{0}}\zeta(B_{s})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s};H_{0}\in\Big[\frac{x}{v}-K,\frac{x}{v}\Big]\Big],

\displaystyle Y_{v}^{>}(x)

\displaystyle:=E_{x}\Big[e^{\int_{0}^{H_{0}}\zeta(B_{s})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s};H_{0}<\frac{x}{v}-K\Big],\quad\text{and}

\displaystyle Y_{v}(x)

\displaystyle:=E_{x}\Big[e^{\int_{0}^{H_{0}}\zeta(B_{s})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s};H_{0}\leq\frac{x}{v}\Big]=Y_{v}^{\approx}(x)+Y_{v}^{>}(x),

where
K>0
is a constant, defined in (
3.16
) below. For
v\in V
and
x\geq 1
we define

\displaystyle\sigma_{x}^{\zeta}(v):=\begin{cases}\big|\eta_{x}^{\zeta}(v)\big|\sqrt{\text{Var}_{x}^{\zeta,\eta_{x}^{\zeta}(v)}(H_{0})},&\text{ on }\mathcal{H}_{x},\\
\sup\limits_{\eta\in\triangle}\left|\eta\right|\sqrt{\text{Var}_{x}^{\zeta,\sup_{\eta\in\triangle}}(H_{0})},&\text{ on }\mathcal{H}_{x}^{c}.\end{cases}

Furthermore, by Lemma
A.1
, there exists some
C>1
such that
\text{Var}_{x}^{\zeta,\eta}(H_{0})=x\big(\overline{L}_{x}^{\zeta}\big)^{\prime\prime}(\eta)\in[x\Cr{constDerLMG}^{-1},x\Cr{constDerLMG}]
for all
x\geq 1
. Thus, there is some constant
=\Cr{constSigman}(\triangle)>1
such that

\Cr{constSigman}^{-1}\sqrt{x}\leq\sigma_{x}^{\zeta}(v)\leq\Cr{constSigman}\sqrt{x}\quad\text{ for all }v\in V,\ x\geq 1,\ \text{on }\mathcal{H}_{x}.

(3.7)

We now prove the following result.

Proposition 3.5
.

Let
V
be as in (
2.22
),
\sigma_{v}
defined by (
3.1
) and
W^{v}_{x}(t)
as in (
3.3
),
v\in V
, and
K>0
be such that (
3.16
) holds. Then there exists a constant
\in(1,\infty)
,
such that for all
v\in V
and all
x\geq 1
, on
\mathcal{H}_{x}
we have

\sigma^{\zeta}_{x}(v)Y_{v}^{>}(x)\exp\left\{xL^{*}(1/v)+\sqrt{x}W^{v}_{x}(1)\right\}\in\left[\Cr{constY_{v}}^{-1},\Cr{constY_{v}}\right].

(3.8)

Furthermore, for all
v\in V
and all
x\geq 1
, on
\mathcal{H}_{x}
we have

\frac{Y_{v}^{\approx}(x)}{Y_{v}^{>}(x)}\in[\Cr{constY_{v}}^{-1},\Cr{constY_{v}}],

(3.9)

and the sequence
n^{-1/2}\big(\ln Y^{\approx}_{v}(n)-nL^{*}(1/v)\big)
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
, converges to a centered Gaussian random variable with variance
\sigma_{v}^{2}\in[0,\infty)
, where
\sigma_{v}^{2}
is defined in (
3.1
) and if
\sigma_{v}^{2}>0
, the sequence of processes

\displaystyle[0,\infty)\ni t

\displaystyle\mapsto\frac{1}{\sigma_{v}\sqrt{n}}\big(\ln Y_{v}^{\approx}(nt)+ntL^{*}(1/v)\big),\quad n\in\mathbb{N},

(3.10)

converges in
\mathbb{P}
-distribution to standard Brownian motion in
C([0,\infty))
.

Proof.

We start with proving (
3.8
) and for this purpose let
x\geq 1
such that
\mathcal{H}_{\lfloor x\rfloor}
occurs. Write
\eta:=\eta_{x}^{\zeta}(v)
and
\sigma:=\sigma_{x}^{\zeta}(v),
and recall the notation introduced in (
2.4
), i.e.
\tau_{i}=H_{i}-H_{i-1}
,
i=1,\ldots,\lceil x\rceil-1
, and set
\tau_{x}:=H_{\lceil x\rceil-1}-H_{x},

x\in\mathbb{R}\backslash\mathbb{Z}
, (which would be consistent with the definition in (
2.4
) for
x
integer) to define
\widehat{\tau}_{i}:={\widehat{\tau}}^{(x)}_{i}:=\tau_{i}-E_{x}^{\zeta,\eta}\left[\tau_{i}\right]
. Then
\sum_{i=1}^{x}E_{x}^{\zeta,\eta}\left[\tau_{i}\right]=E_{x}^{\zeta,\eta}\left[H_{0}\right]=\frac{x}{v}
. We now rewrite

\displaystyle Y_{v}^{\approx}(x)

\displaystyle=E_{x}\Big[e^{\int_{0}^{H_{0}}(\zeta(B_{s})+\eta)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s}\exp\Big\{-\eta\sum_{i=1}^{x}\widehat{\tau}_{i}\Big\};\,\sum_{i=1}^{x}\widehat{\tau}_{i}\in\left[-K,0\right]\Big]\exp\left\{-x\frac{\eta}{v}\right\}

\displaystyle=E_{x}^{\zeta,\eta}\Big[\exp\Big\{-\sigma\frac{\eta}{\sigma}\sum_{i=1}^{x}\widehat{\tau}_{i}\Big\};\,\frac{\eta}{\sigma}\sum_{i=1}^{x}\widehat{\tau}_{i}\in\Big[0,-\frac{K\eta}{\sigma}\Big]\Big]\exp\left\{-x\left(\frac{\eta}{v}-\overline{L}_{x}^{\zeta}(\eta)\right)\right\}.

(3.11)

Analogously, we get

\displaystyle Y_{v}^{>}(x)

\displaystyle=E_{x}^{\zeta,\eta}\Big[\exp\Big\{-\sigma\frac{\eta}{\sigma}\sum_{i=1}^{x}\widehat{\tau}_{i}\Big\};\,\frac{\eta}{\sigma}\sum_{i=1}^{x}\widehat{\tau}_{i}>-\frac{K\eta}{\sigma}\Big]\exp\left\{-x\left(\frac{\eta}{v}-\overline{L}_{x}^{\zeta}(\eta)\right)\right\}.

We define
\mu_{x}^{\zeta,v}
as the distribution of
\frac{\eta}{\sigma}\sum_{i=1}^{x}\widehat{\tau}_{i}
under
P_{x}^{\zeta,\eta}
. Then

\displaystyle Y_{v}^{\approx}(x)

\displaystyle=e^{-x(\frac{\eta}{v}-\overline{L}_{x}^{\zeta}(\eta))}\int_{0}^{\frac{-K\eta}{\sigma}}e^{-\sigma y}\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu_{x}^{\zeta,v}(y)

(3.12)

and

\displaystyle Y_{v}^{>}(x)

\displaystyle=e^{-x(\frac{\eta}{v}-\overline{L}_{x}^{\zeta}(\eta))}\int_{\frac{-K\eta}{\sigma}}^{\infty}e^{-\sigma y}\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu_{x}^{\zeta,v}(y).

(3.13)

Using Lemma
3.6
below, the integrals on the right-hand side of (
3.12
) and (
3.13
), multiplied by
\sigma
, are bounded from below and above by positive constants. Display (
3.8
) now follows by the definition of
W_{x}^{v},
and (
3.9
) is direct a consequence of (
3.12
)-(
3.15
). The last two statements, are a consequence of (
3.8
), (
3.9
),
W_{nt}^{v}(1)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{t}}W_{n}^{v}(t)
and Proposition
3.1
.
∎

To complete the previous proof, it remains to prove the following.

Lemma 3.6
.

Under the conditions of Proposition
3.5
,
there exists a constant
>1
such that for all
v\in V
and
x\geq 1
, on
\mathcal{H}_{x},

\sigma_{x}^{\zeta}(v)\int_{0}^{{-K\eta_{x}^{\zeta}(v)}/{\sigma_{x}^{\zeta}(v)}}e^{-\sigma_{x}^{\zeta}(v)y}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu_{x}^{\zeta,v}(y)\in[\Cr{constCLTHitting}^{-1},\Cr{constCLTHitting}]

(3.14)

and

\sigma_{x}^{\zeta}(v)\int_{{-K\eta_{x}^{\zeta}(v)}/{\sigma_{x}^{\zeta}(v)}}^{\infty}e^{-\sigma_{x}^{\zeta}(v)y}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu_{x}^{\zeta,v}(y)\in[\Cr{constCLTHitting}^{-1},\Cr{constCLTHitting}],

(3.15)

with
\mu_{x}^{\zeta,v}
as in the proof of Proposition
3.5
.

Proof.

We write
n:=\lceil x\rceil
and recall that under
P_{x}^{\zeta,\eta}
, the sequence
\Big(\sqrt{n}\frac{\eta_{x}^{\zeta}(v)}{\sigma_{x}^{\zeta}(v)}\widehat{\tau}_{i}\Big)_{i=1,\ldots,\lfloor x\rfloor,x}
is a sequence of independent, centered random variables. Thus, on
\mathcal{H}_{x}
we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{x}\mathrm{Var}_{x}^{\zeta,\eta}\bigg(\sqrt{n}\frac{\eta_{x}^{\zeta}(v)}{\sigma_{x}^{\zeta}(v)}\widehat{\tau}_{i}\bigg)

\displaystyle=\bigg(\frac{\eta_{x}^{\zeta}(v)}{\sigma_{x}^{\zeta}(v)}\bigg)^{2}\text{Var}_{x}^{\zeta,\eta}\bigg(\sum_{i=1}^{x}\widehat{\tau}_{i}\bigg)=\bigg(\frac{\eta_{x}^{\zeta}(v)}{\sigma_{x}^{\zeta}(v)}\bigg)^{2}\text{Var}_{x}^{\zeta,\eta}(H_{0})=1.

Additionally, the
\widehat{\tau}_{i}
’s have uniform exponential moments. Thus, the conditions of
[
3
, Theorem 13.3]
are fulfilled and an application of
[
3
, (13.43)]
yields

\sup_{\mathcal{C}}\big|\mu_{x}^{\zeta,v}(\mathcal{C})-\Phi(\mathcal{C})\big|\leq c_{1}{n}^{-1/2},

where the supremum is taken over all Borel-measurable convex subsets of
\mathbb{R}
,
\Phi
denotes the standard Gaussian measure on
\mathbb{R}
and
c_{1}
only depends on the uniform bound of the exponential moments of the
\widehat{\tau}_{i}
’s. Without loss of generality, we will assume
c_{1}>4
. Then, due to (
3.7
), by denoting
\mathcal{C}:=\big[0,-K\eta_{x}^{\zeta}(v)/{\sigma_{x}^{\zeta}(v)}\big]
we can choose
K>0
large enough,
so that

\Phi(\mathcal{C})\geq 2c_{1}^{-1}n^{-1/2}\quad\text{for all }n\in\mathbb{N}\text{ and }v\in V.

(3.16)

We thus get

\displaystyle c_{1}^{-1}n^{-1/2}

\displaystyle\leq\Phi\left(\mathcal{C}\right)-\big|\mu_{x}^{\zeta,v}(\mathcal{C})-\Phi(\mathcal{C})\big|\leq\mu_{x}^{\zeta,v}\left(\mathcal{C}\right)\leq\big|\mu_{x}^{\zeta,v}(\mathcal{C})-\Phi(\mathcal{C})\big|+\Phi\left(\mathcal{C}\right)\leq c_{2}(K)\cdot n^{-1/2}.

(3.17)

Because the integrand in (
3.14
) is bounded away from
0
and infinity on the respective interval of integration (uniformly in
n\in\mathbb{N}
), (
3.14
) is a direct consequence of (
3.7
) and (
3.17
). For (
3.15
), we split the integral into a sum:

\displaystyle\int_{{-K\eta_{x}^{\zeta}(v)}/{\sigma_{x}^{\zeta}(v)}}^{\infty}e^{-\sigma_{x}^{\zeta}(v)y}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu_{x}^{\zeta,v}(y)=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\int_{-kK\eta_{x}^{\zeta}(v)/\sigma_{x}^{\zeta}(v)}^{-(k+1)K\eta_{x}^{\zeta}(v)/{\sigma_{x}^{\zeta}(v)}}e^{-\sigma_{x}^{\zeta}(v)y}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu_{x}^{\zeta,v}(y)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\mu_{x}^{\zeta,v}\left(-\frac{K\eta_{x}^{\zeta}(v)}{{\sigma_{x}^{\zeta}(v)}}\left[k,k+1\right]\right)e^{-k\cdot K|\eta_{x}^{\zeta}(v)|}\leq c_{2}n^{-1/2}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}e^{-k\cdot K|\eta^{*}|}\leq\Cr{constCLTHitting}n^{-1/2},

where we recall the notation from (
2.18
).
The lower bound in (
3.15
) can be obtained by noting that

\displaystyle\int_{{-K\eta_{x}^{\zeta}(v)}/{\sigma_{x}^{\zeta}(v)}}^{\infty}e^{-\sigma_{x}^{\zeta}(v)y}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu_{x}^{\zeta,v}(y)

\displaystyle\geq\int_{{-K\eta_{x}^{\zeta}(v)}/{\sigma_{x}^{\zeta}(v)}}^{{-2K\eta_{x}^{\zeta}(v)}/{\sigma_{x}^{\zeta}(v)}}e^{-\sigma_{x}^{\zeta}(v)y}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu_{x}^{\zeta,v}(y)

\displaystyle\geq e^{2K\eta_{x}^{\zeta}(v)}\mu_{x}^{\zeta,v}\big([{-K\eta_{x}^{\zeta}(v)}/{\sigma_{x}^{\zeta}(v)},{-2K\eta_{x}^{\zeta}(v)}/{\sigma_{x}^{\zeta}(v)}]\big).

Analogously to (
3.17
), choosing
\Cr{constCLTHitting}
large enough, the last expression is bounded from below by
\Cr{constCLTHitting}^{-1}n^{-1/2}
. Combining this with (
3.7
), we finally arrive at (
3.15
).
∎

We are now in the position to prove the following result.

Lemma 3.7
.

Under the conditions of Proposition
3.5
, for all
\delta>0
there exists a constant
=\Cr{constY_{v}approx}(\delta)\in(1,\infty)
such that for all
v\in V
,
t>0
, on
\mathcal{H}_{vt}
we have

\displaystyle\Cr{constY_{v}approx}^{-1}Y_{v}^{\approx}(vt)

\displaystyle\leq E_{vt}\Big[e^{\int_{0}^{t}\zeta(B_{s})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s};B_{t}\in[-\delta,0]\Big]\leq E_{vt}\Big[e^{\int_{0}^{t}\zeta(B_{s})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s};B_{t}\leq 0\Big]\leq\Cr{constY_{v}approx}Y_{v}^{\approx}(vt).

(3.18)

Proof.

The second inequality is obvious.
Since
\{B_{t}\leq 0\}\subset\{H_{0}\leq t\}
and
\zeta\leq 0
, we get
E_{vt}\big[e^{\int_{0}^{t}\zeta(B_{s})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s};B_{t}\leq 0\big]\leq Y_{v}(vt)\leq(1+\Cr{constY_{v}})Y_{v}^{\approx}(vt)
by Proposition
3.5

and thus the last inequality in (
3.18
) is obtained. Therefore, it remains to show the first inequality. For this purpose, define the function
p(s):=E_{0}\big[e^{\int_{0}^{s}\zeta(B_{r})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r};B_{s}\in[-\delta,0]\big]
which is bounded from below by
c_{1}(K,\delta)>0
for all
s\in[0,K]
. Using the strong Markov property at
H_{0}
, we finally get

\displaystyle Y_{v}^{\approx}(vt)
