\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\Big(\max_{1\leq k\leq y}\sum_{l=1}^{k}\overline{\rho}_{r_{j}+l}^{(r_{j}+k+m)}\geq L/8\Big)\leq\mathbb{P}\Big(\max_{1\leq k\leq y}\sum_{l=1}^{k}{\rho}_{r_{j}+l}\geq L/16\Big)

\displaystyle\leq c_{11}e^{-\frac{L^{2}}{c_{11}y}},\quad\forall\ y\in[0,t_{i}/2];

here the first inequality is due to (
4.30
) and the last inequality due to (
4.18
).

Putting these bounds together, the second summand in (
4.34
) can be bounded from above by

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t_{i}/2}\frac{\theta L^{2}}{cy^{2}}e^{\frac{\theta L^{2}}{cy}}c_{10}e^{-\frac{L^{2}}{yc_{10}}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=c_{11}\int_{0}^{t_{i}/2}\frac{\theta L^{2}}{cy^{2}}e^{\frac{L^{2}}{cy}(\theta-c_{12})}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y\leq c_{13}\int_{0}^{1/2a^{2}}\frac{\theta}{z^{2}}e^{\frac{1}{cz}(\theta-c_{12})}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}z

\displaystyle\leq c_{14}\theta\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{\frac{x(\theta-c_{11})}{c}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x.

Now the latter term can be made arbitrarily close to zero by choosing
\theta>0
small enough. Furthermore, again choosing
\theta>0
small enough, the first term in (
4.34
) is strictly smaller than one by the central limit theorem from Claim
4.3
. Thus,
\theta>0
can be chosen small enough such that for all
i
large enough, the sum on the right-hand side in (
4.33
) converges, with a finite upper bound independent of
i.

To show
Y^{(n)}\geq n^{-\gamma^{\prime\prime}}
, we adapt the strategy in the proof of (
4.22
), i.e. set
L:=2a\overline{C}\ln n
,
r_{0}=\lfloor\overline{C}\ln n\rfloor
and
J:=\inf\{j:s_{j}\geq\lfloor(\overline{C}\ln n)^{2}\rfloor\}
and keep the other definitions as in (
4.25
) and (
4.26
). Then by the same argument below display (
4.23
),
Y^{(n)}\geq n^{-\gamma^{\prime\prime}}
for some suitable
\gamma^{\prime\prime}>0
follows if
\mathbb{E}[e^{-\theta Y^{(n)}}]\leq c
for some constant
c>0
, some small
\theta>0
and all
n
large enough.
But this follows (as in the argument leading to the definition of
B_{i}^{(n)}
), if the process
(\widehat{R}_{k}-\widehat{R}_{k-1})_{k}
does not decrease too fast, see the Borel-Cantelli argument below display (
4.32
), which itself is a consequence of

\displaystyle\sum_{n}(\overline{C}\ln n)^{2}\cdot\mathbb{P}\big(\sup_{0\leq k\leq L/8\overline{c}}\big(\widehat{R}_{k}-\widehat{R}_{0}\big)\geq\frac{L}{8}\big)

\displaystyle\leq c_{15}\sum_{n}(\overline{C}\ln n)^{2}e^{-a\overline{C}\ln n/c_{15}}<\infty.

(4.35)

This completes the proof of the second inequality in Lemma
4.2
a).

We will now explain how to adapt the latter arguments for the proof of the first inequality in a). We define
\beta_{k}=\overline{\beta}^{(1,n)}_{k}-\overline{\beta}^{(1,n)}_{n-1}
. In the definition of
Z_{i}^{(n)}
, we have to take the essential infimum over
\overline{\xi}(n-t_{i-2})
and have to replace the subscripts
k
of
\beta_{k}
by
n-k
, i.e. “running backwards” from
n
. Thus,
Z_{i}^{(n)}
is
(\mathcal{F}^{n-t_{i}}\cap\mathcal{F}_{n-t_{i-2}})
-measurable. It is then enough to consider the case
n=0
due to the argument in (
4.24
). Writing
Z_{i}:=Z_{i}^{(0)}
,
\beta_{k}:=\beta_{k}^{(0)}
,
\widehat{H}_{k}:=H_{k}-E_{0}^{\zeta,\eta}[H_{k}]
,
\widehat{R}_{k}:=\widehat{R}_{k}^{(0)}
and defining

\displaystyle\overline{\rho}_{k}^{(j)}

\displaystyle:=\underset{\overline{\xi}(j)}{\text{ess sup}}\ \rho_{k},\qquad\overline{R}_{k}^{(j)}:=\sum_{l=k+1}^{0}\overline{\rho}_{l}^{(j)},\quad k<0,\ k\in\mathbb{Z},

we have to adapt the definitions of
r_{j}
and
s_{j}
by the expressions

\displaystyle r_{0}

\displaystyle:=-t_{i-1},\qquad s_{0}:=\big(\sup\big\{k\leq r_{0}:\ \widehat{R}_{k}-\widehat{R}_{r_{0}}\geq L/8\big\}+1\big)\vee(-t_{i}),

\displaystyle r_{j}

\displaystyle:=s_{j-1}-\Big\lceil\frac{L}{8M_{R}}\Big\rceil,\quad s_{j}^{\prime}:=s_{j-1}-\frac{L}{16M_{R}},\quad j\geq 1,

\displaystyle s_{j}

\displaystyle:=\big(\sup\big\{k\leq r_{j}:\overline{R}_{k}^{(s^{\prime}_{j})}-\overline{R}_{r_{j}}^{(s_{j}^{\prime})}\geq L/8\big\}+1\big)\vee\left(r_{j}-(t_{i}-t_{i-1})\right),\quad j\geq 1,

\displaystyle J

\displaystyle:=\inf\{j:s_{j}-r_{j}=-t_{i}\}\vee\sup\{j:s_{j}\leq-t_{i}\}.

The remaining part of the proof essentially follows the same steps as for the second inequality in a).
∎

Proof of Claim
4.3
.

Boundedness, stationarity and adaptedness are direct consequences of the corresponding properties of the sequences
(L_{i}^{\zeta}(\eta))_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}
and
((L_{i}^{\zeta})^{\prime}(\eta))_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}
and Lemma
A.1
. Display (
4.17
) is due to Lemma
A.2
. To show that the central limit theorem, we note that the sequence
(\rho_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}
fulfills the same conditions as the sequence
(\widetilde{L}_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}
in the proof of Lemma
3.2

∎

4.2
Second moment of leading particles

Recall the notation
N_{t}^{\mathcal{L}}
from below (
4.2
) and that of
\overline{m}(t)
from (
1.7
). For the second moment of the leading particles we now prove the following upper bound.

Lemma 4.4
.

For every function
F
fulfilling (
PROB
) and for every
a>0
, there exists
\gamma_{2}=\gamma_{2}(F,a)<\infty
such that
\mathbb{P}
-a.s., for all
t
large enough,

\sup_{x\in[\overline{m}^{(a)}(t)-1,\overline{m}^{(a)}(t)+1]}\emph{\texttt{E}}_{x}^{\xi}\big[\big(N_{t}^{\mathcal{L},a}\big)^{2}\big]\leq t^{\gamma_{2}}.

(4.36)

Proof.

We omit the superscript
a
in the quantities involved and use the same abbreviations as in the beginning of the proof of Lemma
4.1
.

We want to show (
4.36
) with the help of the second-moment formula (
FK-2
). To this end, define the function
\varphi^{\xi}_{t}:[0,t]\to\mathbb{Z}\cup\{-\infty\}
,

\displaystyle\varphi_{t}(s)

\displaystyle:=\varphi_{t}^{\xi}(s):=\lfloor\overline{m}(t)\rfloor\wedge\sup\big\{k\in\mathbb{Z}:s\in\big[t-T_{k+1}-5\chi_{1}(\overline{m}(t)),t-T_{k}-5\chi_{1}(\overline{m}(t))\big)\big\},

where
\sup\emptyset:=-\infty
and
\chi_{1}

has been defined in (
4.2
).
Due to
T_{k}=0
for all
k\leq 0
(recall the notation
T_{k}
from (
3.42
) and (
3.43
)), we have
1\leq\varphi_{t}(0)\leq\lfloor\overline{m}(t)\rfloor.
Furthermore,
\varphi_{t}(t)=-\infty
, because
T_{k}\geq 0
and
\chi_{1}^{\xi}(\overline{m}(t))\geq 0
. To apply (
FK-2
), the following upper bound will prove useful.

Claim 4.5
.

We have

N_{t}^{\mathcal{L}}\leq\left|\left\{\nu\in N(t):X_{t}^{\nu}\leq 0,X_{s}^{\nu}>\varphi_{t}(s)\ \forall s\in\left[0,t\right]\right\}\right|

(4.37)

and
\mathbb{P}
-a.s. for all
t
large enough, the function
[0,t]\ni s\mapsto\varphi_{t}(s)
is a non-increasing, càdlàg step function.

In order not to hinder the flow of reading, we postpone the proof of the latter claim to the end of the proof of Lemma
4.4
.

Figure 3:
Illustration of
\varphi_{t}
, which is the red line. We denote
a_{t}(k):=t-T_{k}-5\chi^{\xi}(\overline{m}(t))
. Note that the sequence
(a_{t}(k))_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}
does not have to be monotone and thus the interval
[a_{t}(k+1),a_{t}(k))
might be empty. In this case the graph of
\varphi_{t}
jumps at least two steps at time
s=a_{t}(k)
.

By the Feynman-Kac formula (cf. Proposition
2.3
) and (
4.37
), we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\texttt{E}_{x}^{\xi}\big[(N_{t}^{\mathcal{L}})^{2}\big]&\leq\texttt{E}_{x}^{\xi}\left[N_{t}^{\mathcal{L}}\right]+(m_{2}-2)\int_{0}^{t}E_{x}\left[e^{\int_{0}^{s}\xi(B_{r})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r}\xi(B_{s})\mathds{1}_{\left\{B_{r}\geq\varphi_{t}(r)\ \forall r\in[0,s]\right\}}\right.\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\left.\times\left(E_{y}\left[e^{\int_{0}^{t-s}\xi(B_{r})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r}\mathds{1}_{\left\{B_{r}\geq\varphi_{t}(r+s)\ \forall r\in[0,t-s]\right\},B_{t-s}\leq 0}\right]\right)_{|_{y=B_{s}}}^{2}\right]\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s.\end{split}

(4.38)

For the first summand we have

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in[\overline{m}(t)-1,\overline{m}(t)+1]}\texttt{E}_{x}^{\xi}\left[N_{t}^{\mathcal{L}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{x\in[\overline{m}(t)-1,\overline{m}(t)+1]}\texttt{E}_{x}^{\xi}\left[N^{\leq}(t,0)\right]\leq c_{1}\texttt{E}_{\overline{m}(t)+1}^{\xi}\left[N^{\leq}(t,0)\right]\leq\frac{c_{1}}{2},

(4.39)

where the first inequality is due to the first inequality in (
3.40
) and the last one due to the definition of
\overline{m}(t)
. Recall that the Markov property provides us with

E_{x}\left[e^{\int_{0}^{s}\xi(B_{r})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r}E_{y}\big[e^{\int_{0}^{t-s}\xi(B_{r})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r}\mathds{1}_{\left\{B_{t-s}\leq 0\right\}}\big]_{|_{y=B_{s}}}\right]=\texttt{E}_{x}^{\xi}[N^{\leq}(t,0)].

Using
\xi\leq\texttt{es}
and the two previous displays, the second summand in (
4.38
) can thus be bounded from above by

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\texttt{es}\cdot(m_{2}-2)\sup_{x\in[\overline{m}(t)-1,\overline{m}(t)+1]}\texttt{E}_{x}^{\xi}[N^{\leq}(t,0)]\cdot\int_{0}^{t}\sup_{y\geq\varphi_{t}(s)}E_{y}\big[e^{\int_{0}^{t-s}\xi(B_{r})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r};B_{t-s}\leq 0\big]\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s\\
&\quad\leq\frac{\texttt{es}(m_{2}-2)c_{1}}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\sup_{y\geq\varphi_{t}(s)}\texttt{E}_{y}^{\xi}[N^{\leq}(t-s,0)]\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s.\end{split}

(4.40)

It thus suffices to upper bound
\sup_{y\geq\varphi_{t}(s)}\texttt{E}_{y}^{\xi}[N^{\leq}(t-s,0)]
by a polynomial in
t.

We treat different areas for
s
and
y
separately and we will need an additional claim, the proof of which will be provided after this proof. It guarantees that the assumptions of the time perturbation Lemma
3.11
are satisfied in our setting.

Figure 4:
Leading particles and the different areas in the proof of Lemma
4.4
.

Claim 4.6
.

There exists
\widetilde{C}_{1}\in(0,\infty)
such that
\mathbb{P}
-a.s. for all
t
large enough and all
y\geq\widetilde{C}_{1}\chi_{1}(\overline{m}(t))
we have
\frac{y}{T_{y}-1},\frac{y}{T_{y}+\overline{K}+5\chi_{1}\left(\overline{m}(t)\right)}\in V
, where
V
is defined in (
2.22
).
Furthermore, there exists
\widetilde{C}_{2}=\widetilde{C}_{2}(\widetilde{C}_{1})\in(0,\infty)
such that
\mathbb{P}
-a.s. for all
t
large enough and all
s\in[0,t-\widetilde{C}_{2}\chi_{1}(\overline{m}(t)))
we have
\varphi_{t}(s)\geq\widetilde{C}_{1}\chi_{1}(\overline{m}(t))
.

We choose
\gamma^{\prime}>5\Cr{const_{t}ime_{p}erturbation}\Cr{constapproxofTn}
. Then, recalling the definition of
\chi_{1}
from (
4.2
) and that
\mathbb{P}
-a.s.
\frac{\overline{m}(t)}{t}\to v_{0}
by Corollary
3.15
, for
t
large enough, the statements from Claim
4.6
hold true, we have
\Cr{const_{t}ime_{p}erturbation}e^{\Cr{const_{t}ime_{p}erturbation}(\overline{K}+1+5\chi_{1}(\overline{m}(t)))}\leq t^{\gamma^{\prime}}
and also
T_{\lfloor y\rfloor+1}\leq T_{y}+\overline{K}
for all
y\geq\widetilde{C}_{1}\chi_{1}(\overline{m}(t))
by Corollary
3.17
.

1)
Let
s\in\big[0,t-\widetilde{C}_{2}\chi_{1}\left(\overline{m}(t)\right)\big)
and
y\geq\varphi_{t}(s)
. Then by Claim
4.6
,
y\geq\widetilde{C}_{1}\chi_{1}\left(\overline{m}(t)\right)
and thus
\frac{y}{T_{y}-1},\frac{y}{T_{y}+\overline{K}+5\chi_{1}\left(\overline{m}(t)\right)}\in V
. By definition of
\varphi_{t}
we have
s\geq t-T_{\lfloor y\rfloor+1}-5\chi_{1}\left(\overline{m}(t)\right)
and thus
T_{y}+\overline{K}+5\chi_{1}\left(\overline{m}(t)\right)\geq T_{\lfloor y\rfloor+1}+5\chi_{1}\left(\overline{m}(t)\right)\geq t-s
. Thus, by Lemma
A.6
, we infer that
\texttt{E}_{y}^{\xi}\left[N^{\leq}(t-s,0)\right]\leq 2\texttt{E}_{y}^{\xi}\left[N^{\leq}\left(T_{y}+\overline{K}+5\chi_{1}\left(\overline{m}(t)\right),0\right)\right],
and then the second inequality in (
3.33
) entails that for all
t
large enough,

\displaystyle\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq s<t-\widetilde{C}_{2}\chi_{1}(\overline{m}(t))\\
y\geq\varphi_{t}(s)\end{subarray}}\texttt{E}_{y}^{\xi}[N^{\leq}(t-s,0)]

\displaystyle\leq 2\Cr{const_{t}ime_{p}erturbation}e^{\Cr{const_{t}ime_{p}erturbation}(\overline{K}+1+5\chi_{1}(\overline{m}(t)))}\sup_{y\in\mathbb{R}}\texttt{E}_{y}^{\xi}\left[N^{\leq}((T_{y}-1)\vee 0,0)\right]\leq t^{\gamma^{\prime}}.

(4.41)

2)
The remaining part of the domain above the graph of
\varphi_{t}
not controlled by
1)
is a subset of

\big\{(s,y)\in[0,t]\times\mathbb{R}:t-\widetilde{C}_{2}\chi_{1}\left(\overline{m}(t)\right)\leq s\leq t\big\}.

Recalling the definition of
\chi_{1}
from (
4.2
) and that
\mathbb{P}
-a.s.,
\frac{\overline{m}(t)}{t}\to v_{0}
by Corollary
3.15
, choosing
\gamma^{\prime\prime}>\texttt{es}\widetilde{C}_{2}\Cr{constapproxofTn}
, on the the above domain we get that
\mathbb{P}
-a.s., for all
t
large enough,

\displaystyle\texttt{E}_{y}^{\xi}\left[N^{\leq}\left(t-s,0\right)\right]\leq 2\texttt{E}_{y}^{\xi}\big[N^{\leq}\big(\widetilde{C}_{2}\chi_{1}\left(\overline{m}(t)\right),0\big)\big]\leq 2e^{\texttt{es}\widetilde{C}_{2}\chi_{1}\left(\overline{m}(t)\right)}\leq t^{\gamma^{\prime\prime}}.

(4.42)

To conclude the proof, defining
\gamma_{2}:=1\vee\gamma^{\prime}\vee\gamma^{\prime\prime}+1
, inequalities (
4.39
), (
4.40
) and the estimates (
4.41
) and (
4.42
) for the term
E_{y}^{\xi}\left[N^{\leq}(t-s,0)\right]
entail the statement of Lemma
4.4
.
∎

Proof of Claim
4.5
.

Let
a_{t}(k):=t-T_{k}-5\chi_{1}^{\xi}(\overline{m}(t))
and recall the definition of
N_{t}^{\mathcal{L}}
:

N_{t}^{\mathcal{L}}=\left|\big\{\nu\in N(t):\ X_{t}^{\nu}\leq 0,\ H_{k}^{\nu}\geq a_{t}(k)\ \forall\ k\in\{1,\ldots,\lfloor\overline{m}(t)\rfloor\}\big\}\right|.

To prove (
4.37
), note that
H_{k}^{\nu}\geq a_{t}(k)
if and only if
X_{s}^{\nu}>k
for all
s<a_{t}(k)
. But the property
X_{s}^{\nu}>k
for all
s\in[0,a_{t}(k))
and all
k\in\{1,\ldots,\lfloor\overline{m}(t)\rfloor\}
implies that
X_{s}^{\nu}>\sup\{k\in\mathbb{Z}:\ s\in[a_{t}(k+1),a_{t}(k))\}\wedge\lfloor\overline{m}(t)\rfloor=\varphi_{t}(s)
for all
s\in[0,t]
and thus (
4.37
) is shown. The property of
\varphi_{t}
being a càdlàg step-function is a direct consequence of the use of left-closed, right-open intervals in the definition of
\varphi_{t}
. It remains to show that
s\mapsto\varphi_{t}(s)
is non-increasing.
For this purpose, let us first prove by induction in
k=\lfloor\overline{m}(t)\rfloor,\lfloor\overline{m}(t)\rfloor-1,\ldots
that for all
t
large enough and all
k\leq\overline{m}(t),

[0,a_{t}(k-1))\subset\bigcup_{l=k}^{\lfloor\overline{m}(t)\rfloor}[a_{t}(l),a_{t}(l-1))

(4.43)

holds. By Corollary
3.17
and Lemma
3.16
, there exist
\overline{K},\Cr{constantlemmaT_{m}teqt}>0
such that
t-T_{\lfloor\overline{m}(t)\rfloor-1}\leq t-T_{\overline{m}(t)}+\overline{K}\leq\Cr{constantlemmaT_{m}teqt}+\overline{K}
and thus
a_{t}(\lfloor\overline{m}(t)\rfloor)\leq 0
for all
t
large enough. Assume now that (
4.43
) holds for some
k\leq\overline{m}(t)
. Then

\left[0,a_{t}(k-2)\right)\subset\left[0,a_{t}(k-1)\right)\cup\left[a_{t}(k-1),a_{t}(k-2)\right)\subset\bigcup_{l=k-1}^{\lfloor\overline{m}(t)\rfloor}[a_{t}(l),a_{t}(l-1)),

where the last inclusion is due to induction hypothesis. Thus, we have shown (
4.43
). Now let
0\leq s_{1}\leq s_{2}
. Assume there exists
k_{2}
such that
s_{2}\in\left[a_{t}(k_{2}),a_{t}(k_{2}-1)\right)
. Then by (
4.43
), there exists
k_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}
with
k_{2}\leq k_{1}\leq\lfloor\overline{m}(t)\rfloor
,
such that
s_{1}\in\left[a_{t}(k_{1}),a_{t}(k_{1}-1)\right)
. By definition we get
\varphi_{t}(s_{1})\geq\varphi_{t}(s_{2})
. If no such
k_{2}
exists, then
\varphi_{t}(s_{2})=-\infty\leq\varphi_{t}(s_{1})
.
∎

Proof of Claim
4.6
.

We write
V=[v_{*},v^{*}]
. Since
\mathbb{P}
-a.s. we have
\frac{y}{T_{y}}\to v_{0}\in\text{int}(V)
by (
3.45
), it follows that
\frac{y}{T_{y}-1},\frac{y}{T_{y}}\in V
for all
y
large enough. Among others, there exists
\varepsilon=\varepsilon(v_{*},v^{*},v_{0})>0
and
\mathcal{N}^{\prime}(\xi)
such that
v_{*}(1+\varepsilon)\leq\frac{y}{T_{y}}\leq(1-\varepsilon)v^{*}
for all
y\geq\mathcal{N}^{\prime}
. Choosing
\widetilde{C}_{1}>\frac{5v^{*}}{\varepsilon}
, this implies
1\leq\frac{T_{y}+\overline{K}+5\chi_{1}\left(\overline{m}(t)\right)}{T_{y}}\leq 1+\varepsilon
for all
y\geq\widetilde{C}_{1}\cdot\chi_{1}\left(\overline{m}(t)\right)
and all
t
large enough. Thus, we get

v_{*}\leq\frac{y}{T_{y}}\cdot\frac{T_{y}}{T_{y}+\overline{K}+5\chi_{1}\left(\overline{m}(t)\right)}\leq v^{*}\quad\text{for all }y\geq\widetilde{C}_{1}\chi_{1}(\overline{m}(t))\text{ and all $t$ large enough}.

This gives the first part of the Claim
4.6
. For the second part, recall that
T_{y}\leq\frac{1}{\underline{v}}y
for all
y\geq\mathcal{N}^{\prime}
. Furthermore, by the definition of
\varphi_{t}
we have
\varphi_{t}(s)\geq\lfloor y\rfloor+1
for all
s\in\left[0,t-T_{\lfloor y\rfloor}-5\chi_{1}\left(\overline{m}(t)\right)\right)
. Choosing
y:=\lfloor\widetilde{C}_{1}5\chi_{1}\left(\overline{m}(t)\right)\rfloor
and
\widetilde{C}_{2}>\frac{\widetilde{C}_{1}}{\underline{v}}+1
, this implies that for
t
large enough we get
\varphi_{t}(s)\geq\widetilde{C}_{1}5\chi_{1}\left(\overline{m}(t)\right)
for all
s\in\big[0,t-\widetilde{C}_{2}5\chi_{1}(\overline{m}(t))\big)
.
∎

4.3
Proof of Theorem
1.4

Recall
\overline{m}(t)=\sup\{x\in\mathbb{R}:u(t,x)\geq 1/2\}
and
m(t)=\sup\{x\in\mathbb{R}:w(t,x)\geq 1/2\}
, where
u
and
v
are the solutions to (
PAM
) and
w
to (
F-KPP
), respectively. We start with an amplification result.

Lemma 4.7
.

For every
(p_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
fulfilling (
PROB
) there exists
=\Cr{const_{a}mpl}((p_{k}))>1
and
t_{0}>0
such that
\mathbb{P}
-a.s., for all
t\geq t_{0},

\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\emph{\texttt{P}}_{x}^{\xi}\left(N(t,[x-1,x+1])\leq\Cr{const_{a}mpl}^{t}\right)\leq\Cr{const_{a}mpl}^{-t}.

Proof.

For the proof it is enough to show the claim for binary branching with rate
\xi(x)\equiv{\texttt{ei}^{\prime}}:=\texttt{ei}(1-p_{1})
(which is the rate of first branching into more than one particle) by a straightforward coupling argument. Due to the spatial homogeneity of
\texttt{ei}^{\prime}
it is enough to show

\texttt{P}_{0}^{\texttt{ei}^{\prime}}\left(N(t,[-1,1])\leq\Cr{const_{a}mpl}^{t}\right)\leq\Cr{const_{a}mpl}^{-t}

for all
t\geq t_{0}
, where
\texttt{P}_{0}^{\texttt{ei}^{\prime}}
is the probability measure under which the branching Brownian motion starts with one particle in
0
and has constant branching rate
{\texttt{ei}^{\prime}}>0
. Then for every
\varepsilon>0
there exists
\delta=\delta(\varepsilon)>0
such that

\texttt{P}_{0}^{\texttt{ei}^{\prime}}\big(|N(t/3,[-\varepsilon,\varepsilon])||\geq\delta t\big)\geq 1-e^{-\delta t}

(4.44)

for all
t
large enough.
Indeed, the probability that the initial particle does not leave the interval
[-\varepsilon t/2,\varepsilon t/2]
before time
t/3
is at least
1-e^{-c_{1}\varepsilon^{2}t}
. If this happens, the particle produces more than
t{\texttt{ei}^{\prime}}/4
offsprings with probability
1-e^{-c_{2}t}
before time
t/3
, while each of these offsprings does not leave the interval
[-\varepsilon t,\varepsilon t]
before time
t/3
with probability at least
1-e^{-c_{1}\varepsilon^{2}t/2}
. Combining these observations and choosing
\delta(\varepsilon)>0
small enough provides us with (
4.44
). For a particle
\nu\in N(t/3)
let
D^{\nu}(t/3+s)
be the set of offsprings of
\nu
in the interval
[X_{t/3}^{\nu}-1,X_{t/3}^{\nu}+1]
at time
t/3+s
,
s\geq 0
. We will show the existence of some
p>0
and
c>1
such that

\texttt{P}_{0}^{\texttt{ei}^{\prime}}\big(|D^{\nu}(t/3+s)|\geq c^{s}\big)\geq p

(4.45)

for all
s
large enough.
To obtain (
4.45
), let
r>0
be such that

\inf_{y\in[-1,+1]}\texttt{E}_{y}^{\texttt{ei}^{\prime}}\big[N(r,[-1,+1])\big]=:\mu>1,

(the feasibility of such a choice of
r
is a direct consequence of the Feynman-Kac formula). For
\nu\in D_{\varepsilon}(t/3)
consider the following process under
\texttt{P}^{\texttt{ei}^{\prime}}_{0}
, conditionally on
X_{t/3}^{\nu}
:

•

the process starts with one particle at position
X_{t/3}^{\nu}
;

•

between times
r(n-1)
and
rn
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
, the process evolves as a branching Brownian motion with branching rate
{\texttt{ei}^{\prime}}
;

•

at times
rn
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
, particles outside of the interval
\big[X_{t/3}^{\nu}-1,X_{t/3}^{\nu}+1\big]
are killed.

Using the Markov property, one readily observe that the number of particles of the latter process stochastically dominates the number of particles of a Galton-Watson process
(L_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
which starts with one particle and whose offspring distribution has expectation
\mu
. Then by
[
2
, Theorem 1, section I.5]
, the Galton-Watson process has positive probability to survive, i.e.
P(L_{n}>0\ \forall n\in\mathbb{N})=:p_{1}>0
. Conditioned on surviving, there exists
c>1
such that

P\big(L_{k}\geq c^{k}\,|\,L_{n}>0\ \forall n\in\mathbb{N}\big)\geq\frac{1}{2}\quad\text{for all }k\in\mathbb{N}.

One can see that for every
\nu\in N(t/3),
inequality (
4.45
) holds true with the choice
p:=p_{1}/2
for all
s\in r\cdot\mathbb{N}
. By a straightforward comparison argument, this extends to all
s\geq 0.
Therefore, we can now apply (
4.44
) and (
4.45
) in order to deduce

\texttt{P}_{0}^{\texttt{ei}^{\prime}}\big(N(2t/3,[-\varepsilon t,\varepsilon t])\geq c^{t}\big)\geq 1-e^{c^{\prime}(\varepsilon)t}.

(4.46)

Furthermore, we have

P_{\varepsilon t}\big(X_{t/3}\in[-1,1]\big)\geq c_{3}t^{-1/2}e^{-3\varepsilon^{2}t/2}\geq\Big(\frac{1+c}{2}\Big)^{-t/3},

for all
t
large enough

\varepsilon>0
small enough and
c>1
suitable, where for the last inequality we used that
\varepsilon
does not depend on
c
. The latter inequality and a large deviation statement then gives for all
t\geq t_{4}\geq t_{3}

\texttt{P}_{0}^{\texttt{ei}^{\prime}}\Big(N(t,[-1,1])\geq\frac{1}{2}c^{t}\Big(\frac{1+c}{2}\Big)^{-t/3}\,\big|\,N(2t/3,[-\varepsilon t,\varepsilon t])\geq c^{t}\Big)\geq 1-e^{-c_{4}t}.

(4.47)

Thus, for
t\geq t_{0}
, where
t_{0}
is chosen large enough, by (
4.46
) and (
4.47
), in combination with
c>1,
we infer the desired result.
∎

With the help of Lemmas
4.1
,
4.4
, and
4.7
, it is now possible to state a crucial result for the proof of Theorem
1.4
.

Proposition 4.8
.

For every
q>0
,
F
satisfying (
PROB
) and
a>0
, there exist a constant
\Cr{constanttheoremlogdist}=\Cr{constanttheoremlogdist}(q,F)\in(0,\infty)
,
a
\mathbb{P}
-a.s. finite
C=C(t)=C(t,q,F,\xi)>0
and a
\mathbb{P}
-a.s. finite random variable
=\Cr{timetheoremlogdist}(a,q,F,\xi)

such that for all
t\geq\Cr{timetheoremlogdist},
we have
C(t)\leq\Cr{constanttheoremlogdist}

and

\emph{\texttt{P}}^{\xi}_{\overline{m}^{a}(t)-C\ln t}\left(N^{\leq}(t,0)\neq\emptyset\right)\geq 1-2t^{-q}.

Proof.

For simplicity, we write
\overline{m}(t):=\overline{m}^{a}(t)
. Without loss of generality, it is enough to show the claim for all
q>2(\gamma_{1}+\gamma_{2})
, where
\gamma_{1}=\gamma_{1}(a)
and
\gamma_{2}=\gamma_{2}(a)
are defined in Lemmas
4.1
and
4.4
, respectively. Let further
\Cr{const_{a}mpl}
and
t_{0}
be as in Lemma
4.7
, and
c_{1}
be such that for
r:=c_{1}\ln t
we have
\Cr{const_{a}mpl}^{-r}=t^{-q}
.

We claim that there exist

\Cr{constanttheoremlogdist},C(t)
and
\Cr{timetheoremlogdist}
as above such that
\overline{m}(t-r)=\overline{m}(t)-C(t)\ln t
,
C(t)\leq\Cr{constanttheoremlogdist}
and the conclusions of Lemmas
4.1
and
4.4
hold for all
t\geq\Cr{timetheoremlogdist}
. Indeed, writing
u(t,x)=\texttt{E}_{x}^{\xi}[N^{\leq}(t,0)]
, by the time and space perturbation Lemmas
3.11
and
3.12
, defining
c_{2}:=\Cr{const_{t}ime_{p}erturbation}\vee\Cr{const_{s}pace_{p}erturbation}
, we deduce that

\displaystyle u(t-r,\overline{m}(t)-\Cr{constanttheoremlogdist}\ln t)

\displaystyle\geq c_{2}^{-1}e^{\Cr{constanttheoremlogdist}\ln t/c_{2}}u(t-r,\overline{m}(t))\geq c_{2}^{-2}e^{\Cr{constanttheoremlogdist}\ln t/c_{2}-r/c_{2}}u(t,\overline{m}(t))\geq a,

(4.48)

where for the last inequality we choose
\Cr{constanttheoremlogdist}
and
\Cr{timetheoremlogdist}=\Cr{timetheoremlogdist}(\varepsilon)
large enough such that the last inequality holds for all
t\geq\Cr{timetheoremlogdist}
. As a consequence, we infer

\overline{m}(t-r)\geq\overline{m}(t)-\Cr{constanttheoremlogdist}\ln t.

(4.49)

By (
3.33
), we also get
u(t,\overline{m}(t-r))\geq\Cr{const_{t}ime_{p}erturbation}^{-1}e^{r/\Cr{const_{t}ime_{p}erturbation}}u(t-r,\overline{m}(t-r))\geq a
for all
t
large enough, i.e.

\overline{m}(t-r)\leq\overline{m}(t).

Thus, combining the two previous displays, we can find
C(t)\leq\Cr{constanttheoremlogdist}
such that
\overline{m}(t-r)=\overline{m}(t)-C(t)\ln t
. Now let
x:=\overline{m}(t)-C(t)\ln t=\overline{m}(t-r)
. Conditioning on whether until time
r
there are more or less than
\Cr{const_{a}mpl}^{r}
particles in
[x-1,x+1]
, we get for all
t\geq\Cr{timetheoremlogdist},

\displaystyle\texttt{P}_{x}^{\xi}\left(N^{\leq}(t,0)\geq 1\right)

\displaystyle\geq 1-\texttt{P}_{x}^{\xi}\big(N(r,[x-1,x+1])\leq\Cr{const_{a}mpl}^{r}\big)-\sup_{y\in[x-1,x+1]}\Big(\texttt{P}_{y}^{\xi}\left(N^{\leq}(t-r,0)=0\right)\Big)^{\Cr{const_{a}mpl}^{r}}

\displaystyle\geq 1-\Cr{const_{a}mpl}^{-r}-\sup_{y\in[x-1,x+1]}\Big(\texttt{P}^{\xi}_{y}\big(N^{\mathcal{L},a}_{t-r}=0\big)\Big)^{\Cr{const_{a}mpl}^{r}},

using Lemma
4.7
in the last inequality.
Now using Cauchy-Schwarz as in (
4.3
), in combination with Lemmas
4.1
and
4.4
, we infer

\displaystyle\sup_{y\in[x-1,x+1]}\big(\texttt{P}^{\xi}_{y}(N^{\mathcal{L},a}_{t-r}=0)\big)^{\Cr{const_{a}mpl}^{r}}

\displaystyle\leq\left(1-t^{-2\gamma_{1}-\gamma_{2}}\right)^{t^{q}}\leq t^{-q},

where we adapt
\Cr{timetheoremlogdist}=\Cr{timetheoremlogdist}(a,q,\xi,q)
such that the last inequality holds for all
t\geq\Cr{timetheoremlogdist}
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.4
.

1) We first prove the result under the additional assumption that
F
fulfills (
PROB
).
Let
w^{\xi,F,w_{0}}
be the solution to (
F-KPP
) with initial condition
w_{0}\in\mathcal{I}_{\text{F-KPP}},
so in particular
0\leq w_{0}\leq\mathds{1}_{(-\infty,0]}
. Because
F
fulfills (
PROB
), by (
McKean
) and the Markov property we infer

\displaystyle w^{\xi,F,w_{0}}(t,x)

\displaystyle=\texttt{E}_{x}^{\xi}\Big[1-\prod_{\nu\in N(t)}\big(1-w_{0}(X_{t}^{\nu})\big)\Big]\geq\texttt{E}_{x}^{\xi}\Big[1-\prod_{\nu\in N(t)}\big(1-{w}_{0}(X_{t}^{\nu})\big);N^{\leq}(t-s,0)\geq 1\Big]

\displaystyle\geq\texttt{P}_{x}^{\xi}\big(N^{\leq}(t-s,0)\geq 1\big)\cdot\inf_{y\leq 0}w^{\texttt{ei},F,w_{0}}(s,y),

(4.50)

where
w=w^{\texttt{ei},F,{w}_{0}}
solves the homogeneous equation
w_{t}=\frac{1}{2}w_{xx}+\texttt{ei}\cdot F(w)
with initial condition
w(0,\cdot)={w}_{0}
. Then we have
w^{1,F,\widetilde{w}_{0}}=w^{\texttt{ei},F,w_{0}}(\frac{t}{\texttt{ei}},\frac{x}{\sqrt{\texttt{ei}}})
with
\widetilde{w}_{0}(x):=w_{0}(x/\sqrt{\texttt{ei}})
. Because
w^{\texttt{ei},F,w_{0}}(0,x)=0
for
x>0
, conditions
[
8
, (8.1) and (1.17)]
are fulfilled.
Together with (
KPP-INI
) and
[
8
, Theorem 3, p. 141]
,
w^{1,F,\widetilde{w}_{0}}
(and thus also
w^{\texttt{ei},F,w_{0}}
) is a traveling wave solution, i.e., there exist
m^{\texttt{ei}}(t)=\sqrt{2\texttt{ei}}t+o(t)
and some
g
fulfilling
\lim_{x\to-\infty}g(x)=1
and
\lim_{x\to\infty}g(x)=0
such that

\sup_{y}\big|w^{\texttt{ei},F,w_{0}}(t,y+m^{\texttt{ei}}(t))-g(y)\big|\to 0,\quad t\to\infty.

(4.51)

Now let
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
and choose
\delta>0
such that
\frac{\varepsilon}{1-\delta}\in(0,1)
. Then by (
4.51
) we get

\inf_{y\leq 0}w^{\texttt{ei},F,w_{0}}(s,y)=\inf_{y\leq-m^{\texttt{ei}}(s)}w^{\texttt{ei},F,w_{0}}(s,y+m^{\texttt{ei}}(s))\geq 1-\delta\ \text{ for all }s\geq s_{0}(F,w_{0},\delta,\texttt{ei}),

which, together with (
4.50
), gives

m^{\xi,F,w_{0},\varepsilon}(t)\geq m^{\xi,F,\mathds{1}_{(-\infty,0]},\frac{\varepsilon}{1-\delta}}(t-s_{0})\quad\text{for all }t\geq s_{0}(F,w_{0},\delta,\texttt{ei}).

(4.52)

The inequality

m^{\xi,F,\mathds{1}_{(-\infty,0]},\frac{\varepsilon}{1-\delta}}(t-s_{0}){\geq}\ \overline{m}^{\xi,\mathds{1}_{(-\infty,0]},\frac{\varepsilon}{1-\delta}}(t-s_{0})-\Cr{constanttheoremlogdist}\ln(t),\quad\text{for all }t\geq\mathcal{T}_{1}(\xi,F,\varepsilon,\delta),

follows from Proposition
4.8
. By Corollary
3.18
, for
C^{\prime}>1
from (
PAM-INI
) we get

\displaystyle\overline{m}^{\xi,\mathds{1}_{(-\infty,0]},\frac{\varepsilon}{1-\delta}}(t-s_{0})

\displaystyle\geq\overline{m}^{\xi,\mathds{1}_{(-\infty,0]},\frac{\varepsilon}{C^{\prime}}}(t-s_{0})-c_{1}(\varepsilon,\delta,C^{\prime})

\displaystyle\geq\overline{m}^{\xi,\mathds{1}_{(-\infty,0]},\frac{\varepsilon}{C}}(t)-c_{2}(\varepsilon,\delta,C^{\prime},s_{0})\quad\text{for all }t\geq\mathcal{T}_{2}(\xi,\varepsilon,\delta),

where the second inequality can be obtained similarly to the argument in (
4.48
) and (
4.49
).
Combining the above inequalities, we arrive at

\displaystyle m^{\xi,F,w_{0},\varepsilon}(t)

\displaystyle\geq\overline{m}^{\xi,\mathds{1}_{(-\infty,0]},\frac{\varepsilon}{C^{\prime}}}(t)-C_{1}\ln(t)-c_{3}

\displaystyle=\overline{m}^{\xi,C^{\prime}\mathds{1}_{(-\infty,0]},\varepsilon}(t)-C_{1}\ln(t)-c_{3}\quad\text{for all }t\geq\mathcal{T}_{3}(\xi).

Now by (
PAM-INI
) every
u_{0}\in\mathcal{I}_{\text{PAM}}
is upper bounded by the function
C^{\prime}\mathds{1}_{(-\infty,0]},
and since we have
\overline{m}^{\xi,\mathds{1}_{(-\infty,0]},\varepsilon}(t)\geq m^{\xi,F,w_{0},\varepsilon}(t)
for all
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
and
w_{0}\in\mathcal{I}_{\text{F-KPP}}
, this finishes the proof for
F
fulfilling (
PROB
).

2) Now let
F
fulfill (
SC
) and
w_{0}
be continuous. By a sandwich argument, it suffices to show that there exists some function
G
fulfilling (
PROB
), such that
F(w)\geq G(w)
for all
w\in[0,1]
. Indeed, by Corollary
A.11
the solutions
w^{F}
and
w^{G}
to

w^{F}_{t}-\frac{1}{2}w^{F}_{xx}-\xi(x)F(w^{F})=0=w^{G}_{t}-\frac{1}{2}w^{G}_{xx}-\xi(x)G(w^{G})

(which are classical by Proposition
2.1
)
fulfill
w^{F}\geq w^{G}
. As a consequence, we infer that
m^{\xi,F,w_{0},\varepsilon}(t)\geq m^{\xi,G,w_{0},\varepsilon}(t)\geq\overline{m}^{\xi,w_{0},\varepsilon}(t)-C_{1}\ln(t)
, where the second inequality is due to step 1). The claim for arbitrary
w_{0}\in\mathcal{I}_{\text{F-KPP}}
is then true by an approximation argument of
w_{0}
by continuous functions, that is if
F\geq G
, by Remark
1.1
we have
w^{w_{0},F}\geq w^{w_{0},G}
and consequently
m^{\xi,w_{0},F,\varepsilon}(t)\geq m^{\xi,w_{0},G,\varepsilon}(t)
for all
t\geq 0
and we can conclude.

It remains to show that for every
F
fulfilling (
SC
) there exists
G
fulfilling (
PROB
) such that
F(x)\geq G(x)
for all
x\in[0,1]
. To do so, recall that (
SC
) implies that there exists
M\in\mathbb{N}
such that

1-F^{\prime}(x)\leq\frac{M}{2}x\quad\text{ and }\quad F(1-x)\geq xM^{-1}\quad\text{ for all }x\in[0,M^{-1}].

(4.53)

Define
G_{n}(x):=\frac{1-x}{n}\big(1-(1-x)^{n}\big)
,
x\in[0,1]
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Then each
G_{n},

n\in\mathbb{N},
satisfies (
PROB
)
with
p_{1}=1-n^{-1}
and
p_{n+1}=n^{-1},

and our goal is to show that
G_{n}\leq F
for all
x\in[0,1]
and all
n
large enough.

We start with noting that
G_{n+1}\leq G_{n}
as functions on
x\in[0,1],
for all
n\in\mathbb{N},
and that
G_{n}\downarrow 0
uniformly as
n
tends to infinity. Thus, since
F
is continuous and
F>0
on
(0,1)
due to
(
SC
), we only have to take care of the neighborhoods of
0
and
1.
From (
4.53
) we immediately get
G_{M}(x)\leq(1-x)M^{-1}\leq F(x)
for all
x\in[1-M^{-1},1]
. To infer the desired inequality for
x\in[0,1-M^{-1}],

Taylor expansion yields

\displaystyle(1-x)^{M}

\displaystyle\geq 1-Mx+\binom{M}{2}x^{2}-\binom{M}{3}x^{3}.

Then for
M
large enough and for all
x\in[0,M^{-1}]
we get

\displaystyle G_{M}(x)

\displaystyle\leq(1-x)\Big(x-\frac{M-1}{2}x^{2}+\frac{(M-1)(M-2)}{6}x^{3}\Big)\leq x-\frac{M}{3}x^{2}

\displaystyle\leq x-\frac{M}{4}x^{2}=\int_{0}^{x}(1-Mt/2)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t\leq\int_{0}^{x}F^{\prime}(t)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t=F(x),

where the last inequality is due to (
4.53
) again.
This finishes the proof.
∎

4.4
Remarks on
v_{c}
and
v_{0}

By the same argument as in
[
10
, Lemma A.4]
, one can show that a rich class of potentials
\xi
satisfies (
VEL
), i.e. the inequality
v_{c}<v_{0}
. It is natural to ask if
v_{c}<v_{0}
is always fulfilled in our setting or not; i.e., do there exist potentials which satisfy (
Standing assumptions
) but not the inequality
v_{c}<v_{0}
?

In order to answer this question, we will take advantage of the following result.

Claim 4.9
.

v_{0}=\inf_{\eta\leq 0}\frac{\eta-\text{\emph{{es}}}}{L(\eta)}.

(4.54)

Proof.

As shown in
[
14
, p. 514ff.]
, the function

I:\,(0,\infty)\ni y\mapsto\sup_{\eta\leq-\texttt{es}}\big(y\eta-L(\eta+\texttt{es})\big)

is strictly decreasing, finite, convex, fulfills
\lim_{y\downarrow 0}I(y)=+\infty
and
\lim_{y\uparrow\infty}I(y)=-\infty
, there exists a unique
v^{*}>0
such that
I(1/v^{*})=0
, and one has

v^{*}=\inf_{\eta\leq 0}\frac{\eta-\texttt{es}}{L(\eta)}.

We now show
v^{*}=v_{0}
.
For this purpose, let
w
and
u
be solutions to (
F-KPP
) and (
PAM
), respectively, both with initial condition
\mathds{1}_{[-1,0]}
. Then
w\leq u
and thus by
[
14
, Lemma 7.6.3]
and Proposition
A.3
we have for all
v>v^{*}
that
\mathbb{P}
-a.s.,

\displaystyle\Lambda(v)=\lim_{t\to\infty}\frac{1}{t}\ln u(t,vt)

\displaystyle\geq\liminf_{t\to\infty}\frac{1}{t}\ln w(t,vt)\geq-vI(1/v).

Since
\Lambda
and
I
are continuous, passing to the limit
v\downarrow v^{*}
we deduce that
\Lambda(v^{*})\geq-v^{*}I(1/v^{*})=0
. Furthermore,
\Lambda(v)<0
for all
v>v_{0}
and thus we infer

v_{0}\geq v^{*}.

To get the converse inequality, we use that for all
v>0
and all
\eta\leq 0
we have

\displaystyle u(t,vt)

\displaystyle=E_{vt}\big[e^{\int_{0}^{t}(\zeta(B_{s})+\texttt{es})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s};B_{t}\in[-1,0]\big]=E_{vt}\big[e^{\int_{0}^{t}(\zeta(B_{s})+\texttt{es})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s};B_{t}\in[-1,0],H_{0}\leq t\big]

\displaystyle\leq e^{(\texttt{es}-\eta)t}E_{vt}\big[e^{\int_{0}^{H_{0}}(\zeta(B_{s})+\eta)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s}\big].

In combination with (
2.9
) this yields that for all
v>0,

\displaystyle\Lambda(v)=\lim_{t\to\infty}\frac{1}{t}\ln u(t,vt)

\displaystyle\leq\inf_{\eta\leq 0}\big((\texttt{es}-\eta)+vL(\eta)\big)=-v\sup_{\eta\leq-\texttt{es}}\big(\frac{\eta}{v}-L(\eta+\texttt{es})\big)=-vI(1/v).

But then
\Lambda(v^{*})\leq-v^{*}I(1/v^{*})=0
and thus we must have
v^{*}\geq v_{0}
.
∎

We now formulate our main result on the relation between
v_{0}
and
v_{c}.
It implies that our results do not apply for all potentials fulfilling (
BDD
), (
STAT
) and (
•
‣
1.4
).

Proposition 4.10
.

There exist potentials
\xi
fulfilling (
BDD
), (
STAT
) and (
•
‣
1.4
), and such that
v_{c}>v_{0};
i.e., condition (
VEL
) is violated.

Proof.

Recalling (
4.54
), the definition
v_{c}=\frac{1}{L^{\prime}(0-)}
from Lemma
2.4
d), it is sufficient to show
L(0)+\texttt{es}\cdot L^{\prime}(0-)<0
, which means

\mathbb{E}\Big[\ln E_{1}\big[e^{\int_{0}^{H_{0}}\zeta(B_{s})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s}\big]\Big]+\texttt{es}\cdot\mathbb{E}\Big[\frac{E_{1}\big[H_{0}e^{\int_{0}^{H_{0}}\zeta(B_{s})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s}\big]}{E_{1}\big[e^{\int_{0}^{H_{0}}\zeta(B_{s})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s}\big]}\Big]<0.

(4.55)

To establish the latter, let
\widetilde{\omega}
be a one-dimensional Poisson point process with intensity one. In a slight abuse of notation,
\widetilde{\omega}=(\widetilde{\omega}^{i})_{i}
can be seen as a mapping from
\Omega
into the set of all locally finite point configurations; i.e.,
\widetilde{\omega}=(\widetilde{\omega}^{i})_{i}
can be interpreted as a random set of countably many points in
\mathbb{R}
, satisfying
|\{i:\widetilde{\omega}^{i}\in B\}|<\infty
for every bounded Borel set and
\widetilde{\omega}^{i}\neq\widetilde{\omega}^{j}
for all
i\neq j
. See
[
32
]
for further details.
Now denote by
\omega=(\omega^{i})_{i}
be the point process that is obtained from
\widetilde{\omega}
by deleting
simultaneously
all points in
\widetilde{\omega}
which have distance
1
or less to their nearest neighbor in
\widetilde{\omega}.

(see
[
26
, p. 47]
for details). Let
\varphi(x)
be a mollifier with support
[-1/2,1/2]
, non-decreasing for
x\leq 0
, non-increasing for
x\geq 0
with
\varphi(0)=1
and let
\varphi^{(\varepsilon)}(x):=\varphi(x/\varepsilon)
,
\varepsilon>0
. Finally, for
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
and
a>0,
define the potential
\zeta(x)=\zeta^{(\varepsilon,a)}(x):=-a+a\sum_{i}\varphi^{(\varepsilon)}(x-\omega^{i})
. One can easily check that
\zeta
is the corresponding
shifted potential as in (
2.3
) of some
\xi

fulfilling (
BDD
), (
STAT
) and (
•
‣
1.4
), i.e.
a=\texttt{es}-\texttt{ei}
. We will choose
a>\ln 2
,
\texttt{ei}\in(0,\frac{a}{8})
and
\varepsilon(a)>0
suitably at the end of the proof. Let us now consider both summands in (
4.55
) separately.

1) We observe that
\mathbb{P}
-a.s.,
\zeta^{(\varepsilon,a)}\downarrow-a
as
\varepsilon\downarrow 0
for all
x\in\mathbb{R},
as well as by
[
4
, (2.0.1), p. 204]

-\sqrt{2a}=\ln E_{1}\big[e^{-aH_{0}}\big]\leq\ln E_{1}\big[e^{\int_{0}^{H_{0}}\zeta^{(\varepsilon,a)}(B_{s})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s}\big]\leq 0

for all
\varepsilon\in(0,1).
Thus, by dominated convergence, for all
a>0
there exists
\varepsilon_{1}=\varepsilon_{1}(a)>0
, such that

\mathbb{E}\Big[\ln E_{1}\big[e^{\int_{0}^{H_{0}}\zeta^{(\varepsilon_{1},a)}(B_{s})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s}\big]\Big]\leq-\frac{3}{4}\sqrt{2a}.

(4.56)

2) To bound the second summand in (
4.55
), we lower bound its denominator by

E_{1}\big[e^{\int_{0}^{H_{0}}\zeta(B_{s})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s}\big]\geq E_{1}\big[e^{-aH_{0}}\big]=e^{-\sqrt{2a}}.

(4.57)

For the numerator, define
\mathbb{J}
to be the set of possible point configurations of the process
(\omega^{i})_{i}
. Let us first check that for all
a>0
there exists
\varepsilon=\varepsilon(a)>0
, such that

\sup_{(\omega^{i})_{i}\in\mathbb{J}}E_{1}\big[H_{0}e^{\int_{0}^{H_{0}}(-a+a\sum_{i}\varphi^{(\varepsilon)}(B_{s}-\omega^{i}))\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s}\big]<\infty.

(4.58)

Indeed, letting
g^{\varepsilon,(\omega^{i})_{i}}(x):=\sum_{i}\varphi^{(\varepsilon)}(x-\omega^{i})
, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}E_{1}\big[H_{0}e^{\int_{0}^{H_{0}}(-a+a\cdot g^{\varepsilon,(\omega^{i})_{i}}(B_{s}))\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s}\big]&=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}E_{1}\big[H_{0}e^{\int_{0}^{H_{0}}(-a+a\cdot g^{\varepsilon,(\omega^{i})_{i}}(B_{s}))\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s};H_{0}\in[n,n+1)\big]\\
&\leq\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(n+1)E_{1}\big[e^{\int_{0}^{n}(-a+a\cdot g^{\varepsilon,(\omega^{i})_{i}}(B_{s}))\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s}\big].\end{split}

(4.59)

Note that by the property of all point configurations in
\mathbb{J}
to have points with mutual distance at least one, we have

\sup_{(\omega^{i})_{i}\in\mathbb{J}}\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}E_{x}\Big[a\int_{0}^{1}g^{\varepsilon,(\omega^{i})_{i}}(B_{s})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s\Big]\leq a\int_{0}^{1}E_{0}\big[\mathds{1}_{A_{\varepsilon}}(B_{s})\big]\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s\leq\frac{1}{2}

for all
\varepsilon(a)>0
small enough, where
A_{\varepsilon}:=\bigcup_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}[-\varepsilon/2+i,\varepsilon/2+i]
. Using Kasminskii’s lemma (cf. e.g.
[
34
, Lemma 1.2.1]
) we infer

\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}E_{x}\big[e^{a\int_{0}^{1}g^{\varepsilon,(\omega^{i})_{i}}(B_{s})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s}\big]\leq 2
. An
(n-1)
-fold application of the Markov property at times
1,\ldots,n-1
supplies us with
\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}E_{x}\big[e^{a\int_{0}^{n}g^{\varepsilon,(\omega^{i})_{i}}(B_{s})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s}\big]\leq 2^{n}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
and all
(\omega^{i})_{i}\in\mathbb{J}
. Plugging this into (
4.59
) we infer

\displaystyle\sup_{(\omega^{i})_{i}\in\mathbb{J}}E_{1}\Big[H_{0}e^{\int_{0}^{H_{0}}(-a+a\cdot g^{\varepsilon,(\omega^{i})_{i}}(B_{s}))\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s}\Big]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(n+1)e^{-na}2^{n},

so the right-hand side in (
4.59
) is finite, and (
4.58
) holds true for all
a>\ln 2
and
\varepsilon(a)
small enough as well. Since
g^{\varepsilon,(\omega^{i})_{i}}
decreases
\mathbb{P}
-a.s. to
0
monotonically as
\varepsilon\downarrow 0
, we infer

\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}\mathbb{E}\Big[E_{1}\big[H_{0}e^{\int_{0}^{H_{0}}\zeta^{(\varepsilon,a)}(B_{s})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s}\big]\Big]=E_{1}\big[H_{0}e^{-aH_{0}}\big]=-\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}a}E_{1}\big[e^{-aH_{0}}\big]=-\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}a}(e^{-\sqrt{2a}})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2a}}e^{-\sqrt{2a}},

(4.60)

using
[
4
, (2.0.1), p. 204]
in the third equality.
Thus, combining (
4.57
) and (
4.60
) we infer that there exists
\varepsilon_{2}(a)>0
such that the second summand on the left-hand side of (
4.55
) is upper bounded by
\texttt{es}\cdot\frac{4/3}{\sqrt{2a}}=(a+\texttt{ei})\cdot\frac{4/3}{\sqrt{2a}}.

Using this in combination with (
4.56
), we infer that for all
a>\ln 2
(which is sufficient for (
4.58
)) and
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{1}(a)\wedge\varepsilon_{2}(a))
, choosing
\texttt{ei}\in(0,\frac{a}{8}),
we get that the left-hand side in (
4.55
) is upper bounded by

-\frac{3}{4}\sqrt{2a}+(a+\texttt{ei})\cdot\frac{4/3}{\sqrt{2a}}<0
.
∎

Appendix A
Appendix

A.1
Properties of the logarithmic moment generating functions

Lemma A.1
.

We recall that
P_{x}^{\zeta,\eta}
has been defined in (
2.6
).

(a)

L,

L_{x}^{\zeta}
, and
\overline{L}_{x}^{\zeta},
for
x\in\mathbb{R}
, (see (
2.7
) to (
2.8
) for their definitions) are infinitely differentiable on
(-\infty,0).
Furthermore, for all
\eta<0
we have

\displaystyle\big(L_{x}^{\zeta}\big)^{\prime}(\eta)

\displaystyle=\frac{E_{x}\Big[e^{\int_{0}^{H_{\lceil x\rceil-1}}(\zeta(B_{r})+\eta)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r}H_{\lceil x\rceil-1}\Big]}{E_{x}\Big[e^{\int_{0}^{H_{\lceil x\rceil-1}}(\zeta(B_{r})+\eta)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r}\Big]}=E_{x}^{\zeta,\eta}[\tau_{\lceil x\rceil-1}],\quad x\in\mathbb{R},

(A.1)

\displaystyle\big(\overline{L}_{x}^{\zeta}\big)^{\prime}(\eta)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{x}E_{x}^{\zeta,\eta}\big[H_{0}\big],\quad x>0,

(A.2)

\displaystyle L^{\prime}(\eta)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\Big[\frac{E_{1}\big[e^{\int_{0}^{H_{0}}(\zeta(B_{r})+\eta)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r}H_{0}\big]}{E_{1}\big[e^{\int_{0}^{H_{0}}(\zeta(B_{r})+\eta)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r}\big]}\Big]=\mathbb{E}\big[E_{1}^{\zeta,\eta}[H_{0}]\big],

(A.3)

and

\displaystyle\big(L_{x}^{\zeta}\big)^{\prime\prime}(\eta)

\displaystyle=\frac{E_{x}\big[e^{\int_{0}^{H_{\lceil x\rceil-1}}(\zeta(B_{r})+\eta)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r}H_{\lceil x\rceil-1}^{2}\big]}{E_{x}\big[e^{\int_{0}^{H_{\lceil x\rceil-1}}(\zeta(B_{r})+\eta)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r}\big]}-\Bigg(\frac{E_{x}\big[e^{\int_{0}^{H_{\lceil x\rceil-1}}(\zeta(B_{r})+\eta)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r}H_{\lceil x\rceil-1}\big]}{E_{x}\big[e^{\int_{0}^{H_{\lceil x\rceil-1}}(\zeta(B_{r})+\eta)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r}\big]}\Bigg)^{2}

\displaystyle=E_{x}^{\zeta,\eta}\big[\tau_{\lceil x\rceil-1}^{2}\big]-\big(E_{x}^{\zeta,\eta}[\tau_{\lceil x\rceil-1}]\big)^{2}=\text{\emph{Var}}_{x}^{\zeta,\eta}(\tau_{\lceil x\rceil-1})>0,\quad x\in\mathbb{R},

(A.4)

\displaystyle\big(\overline{L}_{x}^{\zeta}\big)^{\prime\prime}(\eta)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{x}\text{\emph{Var}}_{x}^{\zeta,\eta}(H_{0}),\quad x>0,

(A.5)

\displaystyle L^{\prime\prime}(\eta)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\frac{E_{1}\big[e^{\int_{0}^{H_{0}}(\zeta(B_{r})+\eta)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r}H_{0}^{2}\big]}{E_{1}\big[e^{\int_{0}^{H_{0}}(\zeta(B_{r})+\eta)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r}\big]}-\Big(\frac{E_{1}\big[e^{\int_{0}^{H_{0}}(\zeta(B_{r})+\eta)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r}H_{0}\big]}{E_{1}\big[e^{\int_{0}^{H_{0}}(\zeta(B_{r})+\eta)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r}\big]}\Big)^{2}\Bigg]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\Big[E_{1}^{\zeta,\eta}[H_{0}^{2}]-\big(E_{1}^{\zeta,\eta}[H_{0}]\big)^{2}\Big]=\mathbb{E}\big[\text{\emph{Var}}_{1}^{\zeta,\eta}(H_{0})\big]>0.

(A.6)

(b)

For each compact interval
\triangle\subset(-\infty,0)
there exists a constant
=\Cr{constDerLMG}(\triangle)>0
, such that the following inequalities hold
\mathbb{P}
-a.s.:

\displaystyle\hskip-11.38109pt-\Cr{constDerLMG}\leq\inf_{\eta\in\triangle,x\geq 1}\big\{L_{\lfloor x\rfloor}^{\zeta}(\eta),\overline{L}_{x}^{\zeta}(\eta),L(\eta)\big\}

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{\eta\in\triangle,x\geq 1}\big\{L_{\lfloor x\rfloor}^{\zeta}(\eta),\overline{L}_{x}^{\zeta}(\eta),L(\eta)\big\}\leq-\Cr{constDerLMG}^{-1},

\displaystyle\hskip-11.38109pt\Cr{constDerLMG}^{-1}\leq\inf_{\eta\in\triangle,x\geq 1}\big\{(L_{\lfloor x\rfloor}^{\zeta})^{\prime}(\eta),(\overline{L}_{x}^{\zeta})^{\prime}(\eta),L^{\prime}(\eta)\big\}

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{\eta\in\triangle,x\geq 1}\big\{(L_{\lfloor x\rfloor}^{\zeta})^{\prime}(\eta),(\overline{L}_{x}^{\zeta})^{\prime}(\eta),L^{\prime}(\eta)\big\}\leq\Cr{constDerLMG},

\displaystyle\hskip-11.38109pt\Cr{constDerLMG}^{-1}\leq\inf_{\eta\in\triangle,x\geq 1}\big\{(L_{\lfloor x\rfloor}^{\zeta})^{\prime\prime}(\eta),(\overline{L}_{x}^{\zeta})^{\prime\prime}(\eta),L^{\prime\prime}(\eta)\big\}

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{\eta\in\triangle,x\geq 1}\big\{(L_{\lfloor x\rfloor}^{\zeta})^{\prime\prime}(\eta),(\overline{L}_{x}^{\zeta})^{\prime\prime}(\eta),L^{\prime\prime}(\eta)\big\}\leq\Cr{constDerLMG}.

Proof.

(a)
Due to the convexity of the exponential function we have
\big|\frac{e^{hx}-1}{h}\big|\leq xe^{hx}\vee 1
for all
x\geq 0
and
h\in\mathbb{R}
. If we choose
h_{0}:=\frac{|\eta|}{2}
, then since
\zeta\leq 0
, we have that for all
|h|\leq h_{0},

\Big|\frac{1}{h}e^{\int_{0}^{H_{y}}(\zeta(B_{r})+\eta)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r}\big(e^{hH_{y}}-1\big)\Big|\leq e^{\eta H_{y}/2}(H_{y}\vee 1).

(A.7)

Due to
H_{y}e^{\eta H_{y}}\leq\frac{1}{|\eta|}
for all
H_{y}\geq 0
and
\eta<0,
as well as
\lim_{h\to 0}\frac{e^{hH_{y}}-1}{h}=H_{y}e^{hH_{y}}
for all
H_{y}\geq 0
, dominated convergence yields for all
\eta<0
that

\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\eta}E_{x}\big[e^{\int_{0}^{H_{\lceil x\rceil-1}}(\zeta(B_{r})+\eta)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r}\big]=E_{x}\big[e^{\int_{0}^{H_{\lceil x\rceil-1}}(\zeta(B_{r})+\eta)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r}H_{\lceil x\rceil-1}\big].

(A.8)

Then (
A.1
) is a consequence of the chain rule and the fact that the expectation on the left-hand side in (
A.8
) is positive. Then (
A.2
) follows from linearity of the derivative. To show (
A.3
), we have to apply dominated convergence once more. This time, we additionally need that the expectation on the left-hand side in (
A.8
) for
x=1
is strictly bounded from below by the constant
E_{1}[e^{-(\texttt{es}-\texttt{ei}-\eta)H_{0}}]=e^{-\sqrt{2(\texttt{es}-\texttt{ei}-\eta)}}>0
due to (
BDD
) and
[
4
, (2.0.1), p. 204]
.
Using the mean value theorem and (
A.7
) entail the existence of
c_{1}>0
such that

\sup_{|h|\leq|\eta|/2}\Big|\frac{1}{h}\big(L_{1}^{\zeta}(\eta+h)-L_{1}^{\zeta}(\eta)\big)\Big|\leq\sup_{|h|\leq|\eta|/2}(L_{1}^{\zeta})^{\prime}(\eta+h)\leq c_{1}E_{1}\big[e^{\eta H_{0}/2}(H_{0}\vee 1)\big]\leq c_{1}\big(\frac{2}{|\eta|}\vee 1\big).

Using (
A.1
) for
x=1
and dominated convergence, we arrive at (
A.3
).

By induction and similar arguments as above, it follows that for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, the function
(-\infty,0)\ni\eta\mapsto E_{x}\big[(H_{y})^{n}e^{\int_{0}^{H_{y}}(\zeta(B_{r})+\eta)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r}\big]
is positive
\mathbb{P}
-a.s. and differentiable. Due to (
BDD
), one can interchange expectation and differentiation in
\eta,
yielding (
A.4
) and
(
A.5
)
. The first equality in (
A.6
) is then again a consequence of dominated convergence. The strict inequalities in (
A.4
) and (
A.6
) are due to the fact that under
P_{1},
and thus also
\mathbb{P}
-a.s. under
P_{1}^{\zeta,\eta}
, the random variable
H_{0}
is non-degenerate.

To show (b), observe that the function
\zeta\mapsto E_{x}\big[e^{\int_{0}^{H_{\lceil x\rceil-1}}(\zeta(B_{s})+\eta)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s}\big]
is nondecreasing. Consequently, using the notation
\triangle=[\eta_{*},\eta^{*}]
, we have

\displaystyle-\infty

\displaystyle<e^{-\sqrt{2(\texttt{es}-\texttt{ei}+|\eta_{*}|})}\leq E_{x}\big[e^{(\texttt{ei}-\texttt{es}+\eta_{*})H_{\lceil x\rceil-1}}\big]\leq\inf_{\eta\in\triangle}\essinf_{\zeta}E_{x}\big[e^{\int_{0}^{H_{\lceil x\rceil-1}}(\zeta(B_{s})+\eta)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s}\big]

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{\eta\in\triangle}\esssup_{\zeta}E_{x}\big[e^{\int_{0}^{H_{\lceil x\rceil-1}}(\zeta(B_{s})+\eta)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s}\big]\leq E_{x}\big[e^{\eta^{*}H_{\lceil x\rceil-1}}\big]=e^{(x-\lceil x\rceil+1)\sqrt{2|\eta^{*}|}},

where we used
[
4
, (2.0.1), p. 204]
for the second inequality and last equality.
Due to the inequality
e^{-xy}x\leq\frac{2}{y}e^{-xy/2}
for all
x\geq 0
and
y>0
, these estimates can be used to derive similar bounds for
E_{x}\big[e^{\int_{0}^{H_{\lceil x\rceil-1}}(\zeta(B_{r})+\eta)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r}H_{\lceil x\rceil-1}^{k}\big]
,
k=1,2
. Thus, estimating the numerator and the denominator of the corresponding expressions in (
A.1
) to (
A.6
), we can conclude.
∎

A.2
Exponential mixing

Lemma A.2
.

Let
\mathcal{F}^{k}
be as defined in (
•
‣
1.4
), i.e.
\mathcal{F}^{k}=\sigma(\xi(x):x\geq k)
, and let
\triangle\subset(-\infty,0)
be a compact interval. Then there exists a constant
C_{\triangle}>0
such that
\mathbb{P}
-a.s., for all
i,j\in\mathbb{Z}
with
i<j
, and all
\eta\in\triangle,

\displaystyle\big|\mathbb{E}\big[L_{i}^{\zeta}(\eta)|\mathcal{F}^{j}\big]-L(\eta)\big|

\displaystyle\leq C_{\triangle}\cdot\Big(\psi\big({\frac{j-i}{2}}\big)+e^{-(j-i)/C_{\triangle}}\Big),

(A.9)

\displaystyle 0\leq\Big(\esssup_{\xi(k):k\geq j}L_{i}^{\zeta}(\eta)\Big)-L_{i}^{\zeta}(\eta)

\displaystyle\leq C_{\triangle}\cdot\Big(\psi\big({\frac{j-i}{2}}\big)+e^{-(j-i)/C_{\triangle}}\Big),

(A.10)

as well as

\displaystyle\Big|\mathbb{E}\big[(L_{i}^{\zeta})^{\prime}(\eta)|\mathcal{F}^{j}\big]-L^{\prime}(\eta)\Big|

\displaystyle\leq C_{\triangle}\cdot\Big(\psi\big({\frac{j-i}{2}}\big)+e^{-(j-i)/C_{\triangle}}\Big),

(A.11)

\displaystyle 0\leq\Big(\esssup_{\xi(k):k\geq j}\big(L_{i}^{\zeta}\big)^{\prime}(\eta)\Big)-\big(L_{i}^{\zeta}\big)^{\prime}(\eta)

\displaystyle\leq C_{\triangle}\cdot\Big(\psi\big({\frac{j-i}{2}}\big)+e^{-(j-i)/C_{\triangle}}\Big).

(A.12)

Proof.

By translation invariance, it is enough to prove (
A.9
) for
i=0
and
j\geq 2
(the case
j=1
follows immediately from the uniform boundedness of
L^{\zeta}_{0}
and
L
on
\triangle
due to Lemma
A.1
). To show (
A.9
), let
\eta\in\triangle
and write
L_{0}^{\zeta}(\eta)=\ln(A+B)
with

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle=A(\eta):=E_{0}\Big[e^{\int_{0}^{H_{-1}}(\zeta(B_{s})+\eta)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s};\sup_{0\leq s\leq H_{-1}}B_{s}<\lceil j/2\rceil\Big]

and

\displaystyle B

\displaystyle=B(\eta):=E_{0}\Big[e^{\int_{0}^{H_{-1}}(\zeta(B_{s})+\eta)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s};\sup_{0\leq s\leq H_{-1}}B_{s}\geq\lceil j/2\rceil\Big].

Then
A\leq E_{0}[e^{H_{-1}\eta}]\leq c_{\triangle,1}<1,
and, using (
2.3
), at the same time we have

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle\geq E_{0}\Big[e^{-(\texttt{es}-\texttt{ei}+|\eta|)H_{-1}};\sup_{0\leq s\leq H_{-1}}B_{s}<\lceil j/2\rceil\Big]\geq c_{\triangle,2}>0\quad\text{for all }j\geq 2.

To bound
B
, we condition on
H_{-1}
to happen before or after time
j
and use the reflection principle for Brownian motion and the tail estimate from
[
7
, Lemma 1.1]
to infer for all
j\geq 2

\displaystyle 0\leq B

\displaystyle\leq P_{0}\Big(\sup_{0\leq s\leq j}B_{s}\geq\lceil j/2\rceil\Big)+e^{\eta j}=2P_{0}(B_{j}\geq\lceil j/2\rceil)+e^{\eta j}\leq 2e^{-j(1/8\wedge|\eta|)}.

As
\ln(1+x)\leq x,
the above implies that for all
j\geq 2

\ln(A)\leq L_{0}^{\zeta}(\eta)=\ln(A)+\ln\Big(1+\frac{B}{A}\Big)\leq\ln(A)+c_{\triangle,3}e^{-j/c_{\triangle,3}}.

Since
L_{0}^{\zeta}
is continuous on
\triangle
, the latter display
\mathbb{P}
-a.s. holds uniformly for all
\eta\in\triangle.

Now
\ln(A)
is
\mathcal{F}_{\lceil j/2\rceil}
-measurable and bounded, so by (
•
‣
1.4
)

\displaystyle\sup_{\eta\in\triangle}\left|\mathbb{E}[L_{0}^{\zeta}(\eta)|\mathcal{F}^{j}]-L(\eta)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{\eta\in\triangle}\left|\mathbb{E}[\ln(A)-\mathbb{E}[\ln(A)]|\mathcal{F}^{j}]\right|+2c_{\triangle,3}e^{-j/c_{\triangle,3}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{\triangle}\cdot\big(\psi(j/2)+e^{-j/C_{\triangle}}\big).

(A.13)

The proof of (
A.11
) is similar. Indeed, using the same notation we have
(L_{0}^{\zeta})^{\prime}=\frac{A^{\prime}}{A+B}+\frac{B^{\prime}}{A+B}
. Then by
A+B\geq c_{\triangle,2}
and
e^{\eta H_{-1}}H_{-1}\leq\frac{2}{|\eta|}e^{\eta H_{-1}/2}
, we can use above calculation to conclude that
\frac{B^{\prime}}{A+B}
decays exponentially to
0
as
j\to\infty
. Further,
\frac{A^{\prime}}{A}-\frac{BA^{\prime}}{A(A+B)}=\frac{A^{\prime}}{A+B}\leq\frac{A^{\prime}}{A}
,
F_{\lceil j/2\rceil}
-measurability of
\frac{A^{\prime}}{A}
and above estimates give a similar bound as in (
A.13
) for
(L_{0}^{\zeta})^{\prime}(\eta)
and
L^{\prime}(\eta)
instead of
(L_{0}^{\zeta})(\eta)
and
L(\eta)
.
Finally, (
A.10
) and (
A.12
) follow by the same arguments as (
A.9
) and (
A.11
).
∎

A.3
Properties of the Lyapunov exponent

Proposition A.3
.

Assume (
BDD
) and (
STAT
)
and let
u^{(x,\delta)}
be a solution to (
PAM
) with initial condition
\mathds{1}_{[x-\delta,x+\delta]}
.
Then the limit
\Lambda(v):=\lim_{t\to\infty}\frac{1}{t}\ln u^{(x,\delta)}(t,vt)
,
v\in\mathbb{R}
, exists
\mathbb{P}
-a.s., is non-random and independent of
x\in\mathbb{R}
and
\delta>0
. The function
[0,\infty)\ni v\mapsto\Lambda(v)
is concave,
\Lambda(0)=\text{\emph{$\texttt{es}$}}
and
\lim_{v\to\infty}\frac{\Lambda(v)}{v}=-\infty
. In particular, there exists a unique
v_{0}>0
such that
\Lambda(v_{0})=0
.

Proof.

Let
x=0
,
\lambda\in(0,1)
and
v_{1},v_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
, and set
v:=\lambda v_{1}+(1-\lambda)v_{2}
and
A:=[(1-\lambda)v_{2}t-\delta,(1-\lambda)v_{2}t+\delta]
.
By (
1.10
), the solution to (
PAM
), admits the Feynman-Kac representation
u^{(x,\delta)}(t,vt)=E_{vt}\big[e^{\int_{0}^{t}\xi(B_{s})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s};B_{t}\in[x-\delta,x+\delta]\big]
.
Then by the Markov property, for all
y\in[vt-\delta,vt+\delta]
we have

\begin{split}\ln E_{y}\big[e^{\int_{0}^{t}\xi(B_{s})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s};B_{t}\in[-\delta,\delta]\big]&\geq\ln E_{y}\big[e^{\int_{0}^{\lambda t}\xi(B_{s})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s};B_{\lambda t}\in A\big]\\
&\quad+\inf_{z\in A}\ln E_{z}\big[e^{\int_{0}^{(1-\lambda)t}\xi(B_{s})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s};B_{(1-\lambda)t}\in[-\delta,\delta]\big].\end{split}

(A.14)

Defining
\overline{\mu}^{\delta}_{s,t}(v):=\inf_{y\in[vt-\delta,vt+\delta]}\ln E_{y}\big[e^{\int_{0}^{t-s}\xi(B_{r})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r};B_{t-s}\in[vs-\delta,vs+\delta]\big]
,
s<t
, by the same argument as in the last display one can see that
\overline{\mu}^{\delta}_{s,t}(v)\geq\overline{\mu}^{\delta}_{s,u}(v)+\overline{\mu}^{\delta}_{u,t}(v)
for all
s<u<t
. Furthermore for the
h
-lateral shift
\theta_{h}
on
\xi
(i.e.
\xi(\cdot)\circ\theta_{h}=\xi(\cdot+h)
) we have
\overline{\mu}^{\delta}_{s,t}(v)\circ\theta_{h}=\overline{\mu}^{\delta}_{s+\frac{h}{v},t+\frac{h}{v}}(v)
for every
h\in\mathbb{R}
, all
v\neq 0
and
s<t
, and by (
BDD
) and a simple Gaussian computation there exists
K_{\delta,v}\in(0,\infty)
such that
\overline{\mu}^{\delta}_{0,t}(v)\geq-K_{\delta,v}\cdot t
for all
t\geq 1
. By (
BDD
) we also have
\overline{\mu}_{0,t}^{\delta}(v)\leq\texttt{es}\cdot t
and thus
\overline{\mu}_{0,t}^{\delta}(v)\in L^{1}
for all
t>0
.
Thus, Kingman’s subadditive ergodic theorem
[
23
]
implies that the limit
\Lambda^{\delta}(v):=\lim_{t\to\infty}\frac{1}{t}\overline{\mu}^{\delta}_{0,t}(v)
exists
\mathbb{P}
-a.s. Furthermore, by (
•
‣
1.4
),
\xi
is mixing and thus ergodic, so the
\Lambda^{\delta}(v)
is non-random. By standard estimates, the limit is independent of
\delta>0
and
x\in\mathbb{R}
.

To show the concavity of
v\mapsto\Lambda(v)
, for
v_{1},v_{2}\geq 0
and dividing by
t
, the left-hand side of (
A.14
) converges
\mathbb{P}
-a.s. to
\Lambda(\lambda v_{1}+(1-\lambda)v_{2})
, while the second term on the right-hand side of (
A.14
) converges
\mathbb{P}
-a.s. to
(1-\lambda)\Lambda(v_{2})
. Dividing by
t
and using (
STAT
), the first term on the right-hand side of (
A.14
) converges in distribution to the constant
\lambda\Lambda(v_{1})
, proving the concavity of
v\mapsto\Lambda(v)
.

By
[
14
, Theorem 7.5.2]
we have
\lim_{t\to\infty}\frac{1}{t}\ln u^{\mathds{1}_{(-\delta,\delta)}}(t,0)=\texttt{es}
, independent of
\delta>0
, giving
\Lambda(0)=\texttt{es}
. Due to (
BDD
) and a standard Gaussian computation we have
\lim_{v\to\infty}\frac{\Lambda(v)}{v}=-\infty
. This, together with
\Lambda(0)=\texttt{es}>0
and the concavity of
v\mapsto\Lambda(v),
implies the existence of a unique
v_{0}>0
, such that
\Lambda(v_{0})=0
.
∎

A.4
A Hoeffding-type inequality for dependent random variables

Lemma A.4

(
[
33
, Theorem 2.4]
)
.

Let
(X_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}
be a sequence of real-valued bounded random variables,
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{i}:=\sigma(X_{j}:j\leq i)
,
S_{n}:=\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i},
and let
(m_{1},\ldots,m_{n})
be an
n
-tuple of positive reals such that for all
i\in\{1,\ldots,n\},

\sup_{j\in\{i,i+1,\ldots,n\}}\Big(\|X_{i}^{2}\|_{\infty}+2\|X_{i}\sum_{k=i+1}^{j}\mathbb{E}[X_{k}|\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{i}]\|_{\infty}\Big)\leq m_{i},

with the convention
\sum_{k=i+1}^{i}\mathbb{E}[X_{k}|\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{i}]=0
. Then for every
x>0,

\mathbb{P}\left(|S_{n}|\geq x\right)\leq\sqrt{e}\exp\left\{-x^{2}/(2m_{1}+\cdots+2m_{n})\right\}.

Rearranging the quantities in the above result, we arrive at the following corollary which we primarily pronounce explicitly since its formulation tailor-made for our purposes.

Corollary A.5
.

Let
(Y_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}
be a sequence of real-valued bounded random variables,
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{k}:=\sigma(Y_{j}:j\geq k),
and let
(m_{1},\ldots,m_{n})
be an
n
-tuple of positive real numbers such that
for all
i\in\{1,\ldots,n\},

\sup_{j\in\{1,\ldots,i\}}\Big(\|Y_{i}^{2}\|_{\infty}+2\Big\|Y_{i}\sum_{k=j}^{i-1}\mathbb{E}[Y_{k}|{\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}}^{i}]\Big\|_{\infty}\Big)\leq m_{i},

(A.15)

with the convention
\sum_{k=i}^{i-1}\mathbb{E}[Y_{k}|\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{i}]=0
. Then for every
x>0,

\mathbb{P}\Big(\Big|\sum_{i=1}^{n}Y_{i}\Big|\geq x\Big)\leq\sqrt{e}\exp\left\{-x^{2}/(2m_{1}+\cdots+2m_{n})\right\}.

Recall that
u^{u_{0}}
denotes the solution to (
PAM
) with initial condition
u_{0}
.

Lemma A.6
.

For all
x\in\mathbb{R}
and
0\leq s\leq t
we have

u^{\mathds{1}_{(-\infty,0]}}(s,x)\leq 2u^{\mathds{1}_{(-\infty,0]}}(t,x).

Proof.

By the Feynman-Kac formula,
\xi\geq 0
and the Markov property at time
s
we have

\displaystyle u^{\mathds{1}_{(-\infty,0]}}(t,x)

\displaystyle=E_{x}\left[e^{\int_{0}^{t}\xi(B_{r})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r};B_{t}\leq 0\right]\geq E_{x}\left[e^{\int_{0}^{s}\xi(B_{r})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r};B_{s}\leq 0,B_{t}\leq 0\right]

\displaystyle\geq\texttt{E}_{x}^{\xi}\left[N^{\leq}(s,0)\right]P_{0}(B_{t-s}\leq 0)=\frac{1}{2}u^{\mathds{1}_{(-\infty,0]}}(s,x).

∎

Lemma A.7
.

Let
N_{s,u,t}^{\mathcal{L}}
be as in (
4.1
).
Then there exists
c>0
such that
\mathbb{P}
-a.s.

\emph{\texttt{E}}^{\xi}_{x}\big[N_{t,t,t}^{\mathcal{L}}\big]\geq c\emph{\texttt{E}}^{\xi}_{\lfloor x\rfloor}\big[N_{t-1,t+1,t}^{\mathcal{L}}\big]\quad\text{ for all }t\geq 1\text{ and }x\geq 1.

Proof.

Write
A_{y,z,t}:=\big\{H_{k}+y\geq z+t-T_{k}-5\chi_{1}(\overline{m}(t))\ \forall k\in\{1,\ldots,\lfloor\overline{m}(t)\rfloor\}\big\}
and
p(r):=\inf_{y\leq 0}P_{y}(B_{r}\leq 0)=\frac{1}{2}
. Then by the Feynman-Kac formula

\displaystyle\texttt{E}^{\xi}_{x}\big[N_{t,t,t}^{\mathcal{L}}\big]

\displaystyle=E_{x}\big[e^{\int_{0}^{t}\xi(B_{s})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s};B_{t}\leq 0,A_{0,0,t}\big]

\displaystyle\geq E_{x}\Big[e^{\int_{H_{\lfloor x\rfloor}}^{t-1+H_{\lfloor x\rfloor}}\xi(B_{s})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s};B_{t}\leq 0,B_{t-1+H_{\lfloor x\rfloor}}\leq 0,A_{H_{\lfloor x\rfloor},1,t},H_{\lfloor x\rfloor}\leq 1\Big]

\displaystyle\geq E_{x}\Big[E_{\lfloor x\rfloor}\big[e^{\int_{0}^{t-1}\xi(B_{s})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s}\times\inf_{r\in[0,1]}p(r);B_{t-1}\leq 0,A_{0,1,t}\big];H_{\lfloor x\rfloor}\leq 1\Big]

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2}P_{x}(H_{\lfloor x\rfloor}\leq 1)E_{\lfloor x\rfloor}\big[e^{\int_{0}^{t-1}\xi(B_{s})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s};B_{t-1}\leq 0,A_{0,1,t}\big]=c\texttt{E}^{\xi}_{\lfloor x\rfloor}\big[N_{t-1,t+1,t}^{\mathcal{L}}\big],

with
c:=\frac{1}{2}P_{x}(H_{\lfloor x\rfloor}\leq 1)
.
∎

The next result is an inequality of Harnack-type flavor for the solution to (
PAM
).

Lemma A.8
.

There exists a constant
\in(0,\infty)
such that

\mathbb{P}
-a.s. for all
y\in\mathbb{R}
,
t\geq 1
and all
u_{0}\in\mathcal{I}_{\text{PAM}}
we have

u^{u_{0}}(t,y)\leq\Cr{Harnack}\inf_{x\in[y-1,y+1]}u^{u_{0}}(t+1,x).

(A.16)

Proof.

For
x\in\mathbb{R}
and
t>0
let
f_{t,x}
be the probability density of a Brownian motion at time
t
, starting in
x
. Let us first show that for all
y,z\in\mathbb{R}
we have

f_{1,y}(z)\leq\sqrt{2/e}\inf_{x\in[y-1,y+1]}f_{2,x}(z).

(A.17)

Indeed, using
f_{t,y}(z)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi t}}e^{-\frac{(z-y)^{2}}{2t}}
, (
A.17
) follows from

\displaystyle\inf_{x\in[y-1,y+1]}\left\{2(z-y)^{2}-(z-x)^{2}\right\}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}2(z-y)^{2}-(z-y+1)^{2}=(z-y-1)^{2}-2,&\quad y<z,\\
2(z-y)^{2}-(z-y-1)^{2}=(z-y+1)^{2}-2,&\quad y\geq z,\\
\end{cases}

\displaystyle\geq-2.

The Feynman-Kac formula in combination with the Markov property applied at time
1
now supplies us with

\displaystyle u^{u_{0}}(t,y)

\displaystyle=E_{y}\big[e^{\int_{0}^{t}\xi(B_{r})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r}u_{0}(B_{t})\big]\leq e^{\texttt{es}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}f_{1,y}(z)E_{z}\big[e^{\int_{0}^{t-1}\xi(B_{r})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r}u_{0}(B_{t-1})\big]\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}z

\displaystyle\leq e^{\texttt{es}}\sqrt{2/e}\inf_{x\in[y-1,y+1]}\int_{\mathbb{R}}f_{2,x}(z)E_{z}\big[e^{\int_{0}^{t-1}\xi(B_{r})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r}u_{0}(B_{t-1})\big]\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}z

\displaystyle\leq e^{\texttt{es}}\sqrt{2/e}\inf_{x\in[y-1,y+1]}u^{u_{0}}(t+1,x),

where in the second inequality we used (
A.17
). Then (
A.16
) follows choosing
\Cr{Harnack}=e^{\texttt{es}}\sqrt{2/e}
.
∎

A.5
Proof of the McKean representation

Proof of Proposition
2.1
.

We start with considering
w_{0}
continuous and
w_{0}(x)\in[0,1]
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
. Define
u:=1-w
and
u_{0}:=1-w_{0}
. Then by
[
21
, Remark 4.4.4]
(providing the existence of a
C^{1,2}
-solution to (
PAM
)) and
[
21
, Corollary 4.4.5]
(Feynman-Kac representation), the function
u^{(1)}(t,x)=E_{x}\big[e^{-\int_{0}^{t}\xi(B_{r})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r}u_{0}(B_{t})\big]
is a classical solution to
u^{(1)}_{t}(t,x)=\frac{1}{2}\Delta u^{(1)}(t,x)-\xi(x)u^{(1)}(t,x)
and we have
u^{(1)}(0,x)=u_{0}(x)
. Furthermore, by the same argument, for all
s<t
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
fixed, the function

\displaystyle u^{(2)}(t,x;s,k)

\displaystyle:=E_{x}\Big[\xi(B_{t-s})e^{-\int_{0}^{t-s}\xi(B_{r})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r}u(s,B_{t-s})^{k}\Big]

is a classical solution to
u^{(2)}_{t}(t,x;s,k)=\frac{1}{2}\Delta u^{(2)}(t,x;s,k)-\xi(x)u^{(2)}(t,x;s,k)
. By dominated convergence, taking advantage of the uniform continuity of the first and second order derivatives, for every fixed
t^{\prime}\leq t
, we can interchange limits to obtain the identities

\displaystyle\begin{split}\sum_{k}p_{k}\int_{0}^{t^{\prime}}\frac{\partial}{\partial t}u^{(2)}(t,x;s,k)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s&=\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\sum_{k}p_{k}\int_{0}^{t^{\prime}}u^{(2)}(t,x;s,k)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s,\\
\sum_{k}p_{k}\int_{0}^{t^{\prime}}\big(\frac{1}{2}\Delta-\xi(x)\big)u^{(2)}(t,x;s,k)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s&=\big(\frac{1}{2}\Delta-\xi(x)\big)\sum_{k}p_{k}\int_{0}^{t^{\prime}}u^{(2)}(t,x;s,k)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s.\end{split}

(A.18)

Conditioning on the first splitting time of the initial particle and on how many children are born, we have

\displaystyle u(t,x)

\displaystyle=E_{x}\big[e^{-\int_{0}^{t}\xi(B_{r})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r}u_{0}(B_{t})\big]+\sum_{k}p_{k}\int_{0}^{t}E_{x}\Big[\xi(B_{s})e^{-\int_{0}^{s}\xi(B_{s})}u(t-s,B_{s})^{k}\Big]\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle=u^{(1)}(t,x)+\sum_{k}p_{k}\int_{0}^{t}E_{x}\Big[\xi(B_{t-s})e^{-\int_{0}^{t-s}\xi(B_{r})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r}u(s,B_{t-s})^{k}\Big]\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s,

where we used the Markov property of the process
(B_{t})_{t\geq 0}
. We deduce that for
h\neq 0,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{h}\big(u(t+h,x)-u(t,x)\big)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{h}\big(u^{(1)}(t+h,x)-u^{(1)}(t,x)\big)

\displaystyle\quad+\sum_{k}p_{k}E_{x}\Big[\frac{1}{h}\int_{t}^{t+h}\xi(B_{t+h-s})e^{-\int_{0}^{t+h-s}\xi(B_{r})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}r}u(s,B_{t+h-s})^{k}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s\Big]

\displaystyle\quad+\sum_{k}p_{k}\int_{0}^{t}\frac{1}{h}\big(u^{(2)}(t+h,x;s,k)-u^{(2)}(t,x;s,k)\big)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s.

Invoking once again the arguments from the beginning of the proof, as
h
tends to zero, the first summand converges to
\frac{1}{2}\Delta u^{(1)}(t,x)-\xi(x)u^{(1)}(t,x)
and by dominated convergence, the second summand converges to
\xi(x)\sum_{k}p_{k}u(t,x)^{k}
. For the third term, we mention that the integrand is uniformly bounded since
u^{(2)}_{t}
is continuous. Again, due to dominated convergence and (
A.18
), the latter term converges to

\displaystyle\big(\frac{1}{2}\Delta-\xi(x)\big)\sum_{k}p_{k}\int_{0}^{t}u^{(2)}(t,x;s,k)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s.

All in all, combining these observations we arrive at

\displaystyle u_{t}(t,x)

\displaystyle=u^{(1)}_{t}(t,x)+\xi(x)\sum_{k}p_{k}u(t,x)^{k}+\big(\frac{1}{2}\Delta-\xi(x)\big)\sum_{k}p_{k}\int_{0}^{t}u^{(2)}(t,x;s,k)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle=\big(\frac{1}{2}\Delta-\xi(x)\big)u(t,x)+\xi(x)\sum_{k}p_{k}u(t,x)^{k},

which is equivalent
w
being a solution to (
F-KPP
). Thus we have shown the claim for continuous
w_{0}
. By Remark
1.1
, for
w_{0}\in\mathcal{I}_{\text{F-KPP}}
the result follows by approximation. ∎

A.6
Existence and monotonicity of solutions

We start with an existence result for generalized and also classical solutions.

Proposition A.9

(
[
14
, Theorem 7.4.1]
)
.

Let
w_{0}
be measurable, non-negative and bounded, and let
F
fulfill (
SC
). Then
\mathbb{P}
-a.s. there exists a unique, bounded generalized solution
w\in C\big((0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}\big)
to (
F-KPP
), which satisfies
0\leq w(t,x)\leq 1\vee\sup_{x}w_{0}(x)
for all
(t,x)\in[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}
.
If additionally
w_{0}
is continuous and
x\mapsto\xi(x,\omega)
is Hölder continuous for
\mathbb{P}
-a.a.
\omega\in\Omega
, then
\mathbb{P}
-a.s. the generalized solution
w
is a classical one, i.e.
w\in C^{1,2}\big((0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}\big)\cap C\big([0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}\big)
and
w
solves (
F-KPP
).

For the next lemma, we introduce the differential operator

(\mathcal{L}_{G}w)(t,x):=w_{t}(t,x)-\frac{1}{2}w_{xx}(t,x)-G(x,w(t,x)),

where
G
is uniformly Lipschitz-continuous in
w
, i.e., there exists a constant
\alpha>0
, such that

|G(x,u)-G(x,v)|\leq\alpha|u-v|\quad\forall\ x,u,v\in\mathbb{R}.

(A.19)

The next lemma is in the spirit of
[
1
, Proposition 2.1]
.

Lemma A.10
.

Let
T>0
,
Q:=(0,T)\times\mathbb{R}
and
G
be such that (
A.19
) holds. Let
w^{(1)}
and
w^{(2)}
be non-negative and bounded functions on
\overline{Q}
, such that for
i\in\{1,2\}
,
w^{(i)}_{x}
and
w^{(i)}_{xx}
are continuous on
Q,
and such that
w^{(i)}_{t}
exists in
Q.
If
\mathcal{L}_{G}w^{(1)}\leq\mathcal{L}_{G}w^{(2)}
on
Q
and
0\leq w^{(1)}(0,x)\leq w^{(2)}(0,x)
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
, then also
w^{(1)}\leq w^{(2)}
on
Q.

Proof.

From the assumptions we infer that

\displaystyle w^{(2)}_{t}(t,x)-w^{(1)}_{t}(t,x)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2}(w^{(2)}_{xx}(t,x)-w^{(1)}_{xx}(t,x))+G(x,w^{(2)}(t,x))-G(x,w^{(1)}(t,x)).

Then, recalling (
A.19
) and letting

v(t,x):=e^{-2\alpha t}\big(w^{(2)}(t,x)-w^{(1)}(t,x)\big),

we get

\displaystyle v_{t}(t,x)-\frac{1}{2}v_{xx}(t,x)

\displaystyle\geq-2\alpha v(t,x)+e^{-2\alpha t}\big(G(x,w^{(2)}(t,x))-G(x,w^{(1)}(t,x))\big)

\displaystyle\geq\big(-2-\text{sgn}(v(t,x))\big)\alpha\cdot v(t,x).

Now the the first factor on the right-hand side is negative and bounded. Applying the maximum principle
[
25
, Theorem 8.1.4]
to
-v
then implies that
v\geq 0
on
Q
and we can conclude.
∎

As an important application we get that the solution
w
to
\mathcal{L}_{G}w=0
is monotone in
G
and in the initial condition.

Corollary A.11
.

Let
T>0
,
Q:=(0,T)\times\mathbb{R}
, and let
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
fulfill
{G_{1}}\leq{G_{2}}
on
\mathbb{R}\times[0,\infty).
Furthermore, assume that
G_{2}
satisfies (
A.19
). In addition, let
w^{(1)}
and
w^{(2)}
be non-negative and bounded functions on
\overline{Q}
, such that for
i\in\{1,2\}
,
w^{(i)}_{x}
and
w^{(i)}_{xx}
are continuous on
Q
and
w^{(i)}_{t}
exist on
Q
. If
\mathcal{L}_{G_{1}}w^{(1)}=\mathcal{L}_{G_{2}}w^{(2)}
and
w^{(1)}(0,\cdot)\leq w^{(2)}(0,\cdot)
on
x\in\mathbb{R}
, then we have
w^{(1)}\leq w^{(2)}
on
Q.

Proof.

Since the function
w^{(2)}
is non-negative, we have
\mathcal{L}_{G_{2}}w^{(2)}=\mathcal{L}_{G_{1}}w^{(1)}\geq\mathcal{L}_{G_{2}}w^{(1)}
. Then by Lemma
A.10
, we have
w^{(1)}(t,x)\leq w^{(2)}(t,x)
for all
(t,x)\in Q
.
∎

Corollary A.12
.

Let
G
fulfill (
A.19
) and
G(x,0)=G(x,1)=0
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
. Let
w
be a solution to
\mathcal{L}_{G}w=0
with
0\leq w(0,x)\leq 1
. Then
0\leq w(t,x)\leq 1
for all
(t,x)\in[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}
.

Proof.

The functions
w^{(1)}(t,x)=0
and
w^{(2)}(t,x)=1
are solutions to
\mathcal{L}w^{(1)}=\mathcal{L}w^{(2)}=0
and
w^{(1)}(0,x)\leq w(0,x)\leq w^{(2)}(0,x)
. The claim then follows from Lemma
A.10
.
∎
